Z_{s,N,R}e^{R_{s,N}(u)}\rho_{s,R,N}(A)=\mu_{s}(A)

Indeed, it results from

\begin{split}Z_{s,N,R}e^{R_{s,N}(u)}\rho_{s,R,N}(A)&=\int_{A}Z_{s,N,R}e^{R_{s,N}(u)}d\rho_{s,R,N}(u)\\
&=\int_{A}Z_{s,N,R}e^{R_{s,N}(u)}\underbrace{\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}}_{=1}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}\frac{1}{Z_{s,N,R}}d\mu_{s}(u)\\
&=\int_{A}d\mu_{s}(u)=\mu_{s}(A)\end{split}

Now, we state the following crucial proposition, whose proof is postponed to the dedicated Section
11
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
and
R>0
. Then for any
p\in[1,+\infty)
, there exists a constant
C(s,p,R)>0
such that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, we have:

(7.2)

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{|R_{s,N}(u)|}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\leq C(s,p,R)

Moreover,

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s}(u)}-\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

In particular,
Z_{s,N,R}\underset{N}{\longrightarrow}Z_{s,R}
so that we also have:

(7.3)

\left\lVert\frac{1}{Z_{s,R}}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s}(u)}-\frac{1}{Z_{s,R,N}}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Remark 7.5
.

The inequality (
7.2
) in Proposition
7.4
ensures that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
:

Z_{s,R,N}=\int_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}d\mu_{s}<+\infty

Besides, we also have
Z_{s,R,N}>0
because one can show that
\mu_{s}(\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}>0)>0
. To see this, we write on the one hand:

\mu_{s}\left(\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}>0\right)=\mu_{s}\left(\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}\cap R_{s,N}<+\infty\right)=\mu_{s}\left(\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}\right)

and on the other hand, we have that
\mu_{s}(\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\})>0
, for any
R>0
, since
\mu_{s}
charges all open sets of
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
. For this latter point, we refer to
[
7
]
, Proposition 1.2.
Note also that since
Z_{s,R,N}\rightarrow Z_{s,R}
, there exists a constant
C_{s,R}>0
such that:

(7.4)

\frac{1}{C_{s,R}}\leq Z_{s,R,N}\leq C_{s,R}

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

7.2.
Transport of weighted Gaussian measures along the flows

For
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, now that we know explicitly the density of
\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}\mu_{s}
with respect to
\mu_{s}
, we are able to obtain the density of
\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}\rho_{s,R,N}
with respect to
\rho_{s,R,N}
without re-performing the analysis of Section
5
and
6
.

We stress the fact that the following proposition holds for
N\in\mathbb{N}

and

N=\infty
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
,
R>0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. For every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have:

\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}\rho_{s,R,N}=F_{s,N}(t,u)d\rho_{s,R,N}

for a function
F_{s,N}(t,.)
given by the explicit formula:

F_{s,N}(t,u)=\textnormal{exp}\left(-\int_{0}^{-t}Q_{s,N}(\Phi_{N}(\tau)u)d\tau\right)

where
Q_{s,N}
is defined in (
4.4
) (see also Definition
2.3
). In particular,
\rho_{s,R}
is quasi-invariant along the flow of (
1.1
); and when
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho_{s,R,N}
is quasi-invariant along the flow of (
2.1
).

Remark 7.7
.

When
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s,N}(\Phi_{N}(\tau)u)d\tau=E_{s,N}(\Pi_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)u)-E_{s,N}(\Pi_{N}u)

Hence, the a priori ill-defined object
E_{s}(\Phi(t)u)-E_{s}(u)
(on the support of
\mu_{s}
) can be seen as:

E_{s}(\Phi(t)u)-E_{s}(u):=\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s}(\Phi(\tau)u)d\tau

which is a continuous function on
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
for
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
close enough to
s-\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof of Proposition
7.6
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. We start by applying a general feature for the transport of density measures:

\begin{split}\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}\rho_{s,R,N}&=\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}\left(\frac{1}{Z_{s,R,N}}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}d\mu_{s}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{Z_{s,R,N}}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(\Phi_{N}(t)^{-1}u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(\Phi_{N}(t)^{-1}u)}\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}d\mu_{s}\end{split}

Next, thanks to Proposition
5.3
and
6.1
, along with the facts that
\Phi_{N}(t)^{-1}=\Phi_{N}(-t)
and that
\mathcal{C}
is conserved by the flows, we obtain:

\begin{split}\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}\rho_{s,R,N}&=\frac{1}{Z_{s,R,N}}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\textnormal{exp}\left(-R_{s,N}(u)-\int_{0}^{-t}Q_{s,N}(\Phi_{N}(\tau)u)d\tau\right)d\mu_{s}\\
&=\textnormal{exp}\left(-\int_{0}^{-t}Q_{s,N}(\Phi_{N}(\tau)u)d\tau\right)d\rho_{s,R,N}\end{split}

which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
and
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
. Then, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
F_{s,N}(t,.)
converges to
F_{s}(t,.)
uniformly on compact sets of
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
(see the proof of Proposition
6.5
).

8.
Densities in
L^{p}
and convergence in
L^{p}
of the truncated densities

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
. In Section
5
and
6
, we have seen that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and every
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}\mu_{s}

\displaystyle=G_{s,N}(t,.)\mu_{s},

and,

\displaystyle\Phi(t)_{\#}\mu_{s}

\displaystyle=G_{s}(t,.)\mu_{s}

where
G_{s,N}
and
G_{s}
are known explicitly (see Proposition
5.3
and
6.1
). In this section, our goal is to prove that for every fixed
R>0
, and every
t\in\mathbb{R}
:

G_{s}(t,.),\hskip 2.84544ptG_{s,N}(t,.)\in\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s,R})\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and:}\hskip 5.69046pt\left\lVert G_{s}(t,.)-G_{s,N}(t,.)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s,R})}\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

where
\mu_{s,R}
is the
restricted Gaussian measure
define by:

(8.1)

\mu_{s,R}:=\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mu_{s}

This is equivalent to the fact that:

\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}G_{s}(t,.),\hskip 2.84544pt\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}G_{s,N}(t,.)\in\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and:}\hskip 5.69046pt\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}(G_{s}(t,.)-G_{s,N}(t,.))\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Remark 8.1
.

Since
\mathcal{C}
is conserved by the flow, we still have:

(8.2)

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}\mu_{s,R}

\displaystyle=G_{s,N}(t,.)\mu_{s,R},

and,

\displaystyle\Phi(t)_{\#}\mu_{s,R}

\displaystyle=G_{s}(t,.)\mu_{s,R}

In our approach, we do not consider directly
\mu_{s,R}
but we consider instead the weighted Gaussian measures
\rho_{s,R,N}
. We will then be able to prove a
quantitative inequality
in Proposition
8.5
thanks to suitable
\mathbb{L}^{p}
-estimates on
Q_{s,N}
.
In a second step, we will be able to go back to
\mu_{s,R}
by proving the same quantitative inequaltiy for
\mu_{s,R}
.

The quantitative inequaltiy (
8.6
) is significant in itself; indeed, it is often used to obtain the quasi-invariance without knowing the Radon-Nikodym derivative, see for example
[
13
]
,
[
23
]
,
[
33
]
,
[
15
]
. Here, we know explicitly the Radon-Nikodym derivative of the transported measure, and from this point it will be easier to establish (
8.6
).

8.1.
Quantitative quasi-invariance

Recall that for convenience we use the notations
\rho_{s,R,\infty}=\rho_{s,R},Q_{s}=Q_{s,\infty},\text{etc}
.

We start this paragraph by providing
L^{p}
estimates for
Q_{s,N}
. These estimates will help us to establish the
quantitative quasi-invariance property
in Proposition
8.5
. We postpone the proof of the following proposition to Section
12
where a detailed analysis is provided.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
. There exists
\beta\in(0,1)
such that for every
R>0
, there exists a constant
C(s,R)>0
, such that for any
p\in[2,+\infty)
,

(8.3)

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}{u}\leq R\}}Q_{s,N}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\leq C(s,R)p^{\beta}

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Combining estimate (
8.3
) with estimate (
7.2
) from Proposition
7.4
, we also have:

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
. There exists
\beta\in(0,1)
such that for every
R>0
, there exists a constant
C(s,R)>0
, such that for any
p\in[1,+\infty)
,

(8.4)

\left\lVert Q_{s,N}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\rho_{s,R,N})}\leq C(s,R)p^{\beta}

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Proof of Proposition
8.3
assuming (
8.3
) and (
7.2
).

It results from Cauchy-Schwarz that:

\begin{split}\left\lVert Q_{s,N}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\rho_{s,R,N})}&=\left\lVert\frac{1}{Z_{s,R,N}}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}{u}\leq R\}}|Q_{s,N}(u)|^{p}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{1}(d\mu_{s})}^{1/p}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{Z_{s,R,N}}\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}{u}\leq R\}}Q_{s,N}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2p}(d\mu_{s})}\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}{u}\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(d\mu_{s})}^{1/p}\\
&\leq C(s,R)2^{\beta}p^{\beta}\cdot C(s,R,2)^{1/p}\leq C(s,R)p^{\beta}\end{split}

where the constant
C(s,R)
has changed but still depends only on
s
and
R
. Note also that we used (
7.4
).
∎

The following identity will be our starting point in order to obtain the forthcoming inequality (
8.6
).

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
,
R>0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. For every Borel set
A\subset H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
, we have :

(8.5)

\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{s,R,N}\left(\Phi_{N}(t)(A)\right)=-\int_{\Phi_{N}(t)A}Q_{s,N}(u)d\rho_{s,R,N}

Proof of Proposition
8.4
.

We use the explicit formula for the density from Proposition
7.6
, so that we obtain:

\begin{split}&\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{s,R,N}\left(\Phi_{N}(t)(A)\right)=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\Phi_{N}(-t)_{\#}\rho_{s,R,N}\right)(A)=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{A}e^{-\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s,N}(\Phi_{N}(\tau)u)d\tau}d\rho_{s,R,N}\\
&=-\int_{A}Q_{s,N}(\Phi_{N}(t)u)d\left(\Phi_{N}(-t)_{\#}\rho_{s,R,N}\right)(u)=-\int_{\Phi_{N}(t)A}Q_{s,N}(u)d\rho_{s,R,N}\end{split}

where the last equality follows from the definition of a push-forward measure.
∎

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
,
R>0
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, there exists
\beta\in(0,1)
such that for every
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C=C_{s,R,\alpha,\beta}>0
such that for all Borel set
A\subset H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
:

(8.6)

\rho_{s,R,N}(\Phi_{N}(t)A)\leq\rho_{s,R,N}(A)^{1-\alpha}\textnormal{exp}\left(C(1+|t|)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\right)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Proof.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
,
R>0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Let
A\subset H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
be a Borel set. Using (
8.5
), Hölder inequality and the energy estimate from Proposition
8.3
, we obtain :

\big|\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{s,R,N}(\Phi_{N}(t)A)\big|\leq\left\lVert Q_{s,N}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\rho_{s,R,N})}\rho_{s,R,N}(\Phi_{N}(t)A)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\leq C_{s,R}\hskip 2.84544ptp^{\beta}\rho_{s,R,N}(\Phi_{N}(t)A)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}

for all
p\in[1,+\infty)
. It means that the function
F(t):=\rho_{s,R,N}(\Phi_{N}(t)A)
satisfies the differential inequality :

|F^{\prime}(t)|\leq C_{s,R}\hskip 2.84544ptp^{\beta}F(t)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}

Integrating this yields :

\begin{split}F(t)\leq\left(F(0)^{\frac{1}{p}}+C_{s,R}|t|p^{\beta-1}\right)^{p}&=F(0)\left(1+C_{s,R}|t|F(0)^{-\frac{1}{p}}p^{\beta-1}\right)^{p}\\
&=F(0)\textnormal{exp}\left(p\textnormal{log}(1+C_{s,R}|t|F(0)^{-\frac{1}{p}}p^{\beta-1})\right)\end{split}

Then, using the inequality
\textnormal{log}(1+x)\leq x
implies :

F(t)\leq F(0)\textnormal{exp}\left(C_{s,R}|t|F(0)^{-\frac{1}{p}}p^{\beta})\right)

Now, we choose
p:=1+\textnormal{log}(\frac{1}{F(0)})
so that
F(0)^{-\frac{1}{p}}=\textnormal{exp}(\frac{\textnormal{log}(F(0))}{\textnormal{log}(F(0))-1})\leq\textnormal{exp}(1)
. Hence,

(8.7)

F(t)\leq F(0)\textnormal{exp}\left(C_{s,R}|t|(1-\textnormal{log}F(0))^{\beta}\right)

Let us mention the following elementary lemma, whose proof is provided afterwards.

Lemma 8.6
.

For every
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C_{s,R,\alpha,\beta}>0
such that for all
x\geq 0
:

C_{s,R}|t|\left(1+x\right)^{\beta}\leq\alpha x+C_{s,R,\alpha,\beta}\left(1+|t|\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}

Using Lemma
8.6
in (
8.7
) with
x=-\textnormal{log}(F(0))\geq 0
yields :

F(t)\leq F(0)^{1-\alpha}\textnormal{exp}\left(C_{s,R,\alpha,\beta}(1+|t|)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\right)

which is the desired inequality, so the proof is completed.
∎

Here, we provide a proof of Lemma
8.6
:

Proof of Lemma
8.6
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
. We invoke the function
f(x):=C_{s,R}|t|(1+x)^{\beta}-\alpha x
. We aim to show that
f(x)\leq C_{s,R,\alpha,\beta}\left(1+|t|\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}
. Recall that
\beta\in(0,1)
. We have :

f^{\prime}(x)=\beta C_{s,R}|t|(1+x)^{\beta-1}-\alpha\geq 0\iff(1+x)^{\beta-1}\geq\frac{\alpha}{\beta C_{s,R}|t|}\iff x\leq\left(\frac{\beta C_{s,R}|t|}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}-1

This implies that
f
has a maximum at the point
x=\left(\frac{\beta C_{s,R}|t|}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}-1
. Thus, for all
x\geq 0
:

\begin{split}f(x)&\leq C_{s,R}|t|\left(\frac{\beta C_{s,R}|t|}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}}-\alpha\left(\frac{\beta C_{s,R}|t|}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}+\alpha\\
&=\alpha+(C_{s,R}|t|)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\left(\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}}-\alpha\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\right)\\
&\leq C_{s,R,\alpha,\beta}(1+|t|)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\end{split}

This completes the proof of Lemma
8.6
.
∎

Now, we can go back to the measure
\mu_{s,R}
(see (
8.1
) for the definition). Indeed, we are able to deduce from Proposition
8.5
the analogous proposition for
\mu_{s,R}
.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
,
R>0
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, there exists
\beta\in(0,1)
such that for every
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C=C_{s,R,\alpha,\beta}>0
such that for all Borel set
A\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
:

(8.8)

\mu_{s,R}(\Phi_{N}(t)A)\leq\mu_{s,R}(A)^{1-\alpha}\textnormal{exp}\left(C(1+|t|)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\right)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Proof.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Recall that
\mu_{s,R}
is defined in (
8.1
), and observe that we have:

\mu_{s,R}=Z_{s,N,R}e^{R_{s,N}(u)}d\rho_{s,R,N}

(see also Remark
7.3
). Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Let us invoke the
\beta\in(0,1)
and the constant
C>0
from Proposition
8.5
. Then, for every
q_{1}\in(1,\infty)
:

\begin{split}\mu_{s,R}(\Phi_{N}(t)A)=Z_{s,R,N}\int_{\Phi(t)A}e^{R_{s,N}(u)}d\rho_{s,R,N}&\leq Z_{s,R,N}\left\lVert e^{R_{s,N}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{q_{1}}(d\rho_{s,R,N})}\rho_{s,R,N}(\Phi(t)A)^{1-\frac{1}{q_{1}}}\\
&\leq C_{s,R,q_{1}}\rho_{s,R,N}(\Phi(t)A)^{1-\frac{1}{q_{1}}}\end{split}

where in the last inequality we used (
7.4
) and Proposition
7.4
. Using now Proposition
8.5
, we obtain:

(8.9)

\mu_{s,R}(\Phi_{N}(t)A)\leq\rho_{s,R,N}(A)^{(1-\alpha)(1-\frac{1}{q_{1}})}\textnormal{exp}\left(C(1+|t|)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\right)

where the constant
C>0
depends on
s,R,\alpha
and
q_{1}
(and also
\beta
). On the other hand, for every
q_{2}\in(1,\infty)
:

\begin{split}\rho_{s,R,N}(A)=\frac{1}{Z_{s,R,N}}\int_{A}e^{-R_{s,N}}d\mu_{s,R}&\leq\frac{1}{Z_{s,R,N}}\left\lVert e^{-R_{s,N}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{q_{2}}(d\mu_{s,R})}\mu_{s,R}(A)^{1-\frac{1}{q_{2}}}\\
&\leq C_{s,R,q_{2}}\mu_{s,R}(A)^{1-\frac{1}{q_{2}}}\end{split}

where again in the last inequality we used (
7.4
) and Proposition
7.4
. Then, plugging this into (
8.9
) yields:

\mu_{s,R}(\Phi_{N}(t)A)\leq\rho_{s,R,N}(A)^{(1-\alpha)(1-\frac{1}{q_{1}})(1-\frac{1}{q_{2}})}\textnormal{exp}\left(C(1+|t|)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\right)

where now, the constant
C>0
depends on
s,R,\alpha,q_{1}
and
q_{2}
. Finally, since
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in(1,\infty)
are arbitrary, we have that
(1-\alpha)(1-\frac{1}{q_{1}})(1-\frac{1}{q_{2}})
takes all the values in
(0,1)
. This completes the proof.
∎

8.2.
Consequences: uniform
L^{p}
integrability and convergence

In this paragraph, we perform an analysis similar to the one from Section 7 of
[
15
]
. As a consequence of Proposition
8.7
, we are now able to prove the following proposition. Here, for clarity, we denote
G_{s,N,t}=G_{s,N}(t,.)
for
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
(still
G_{s,\infty,t}=G_{s,t}
).

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
,
R>0
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. For every
p\in[1,+\infty)
, there exists a constant
C(s,R,p,t)\in(0,+\infty)
such that :

\left\lVert G_{s,N,t}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s,R})}\leq C(s,R,p,t)

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Proof.

Let
p\in[1,+\infty)
and let us prove that
G_{s,N,t}
belongs to
\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s,R})
. To do so, we use Cavalieri’s principle :

(8.10)

\left\lVert G_{s,N,t}\right\rVert_{L^{p}(d\mu_{s,R})}^{p}=p\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{p-1}\mu_{s,R}(G_{s,N,t}>\lambda)d\lambda

We can estimate
\mu_{s,R}(G_{s,N,t}>\lambda)
as follows :

\begin{split}&\mu_{s,R}(G_{s,N,t}>\lambda)=\int\mathds{1}_{\{G_{s,N,t}>\lambda\}}d\mu_{s,R}=\int\frac{1}{G_{s,N,t}}\mathds{1}_{\{G_{s,N,t}>\lambda\}}G_{s,N,t}d\mu_{s,R}\\
&=\int\frac{1}{G_{s,N,t}}\mathds{1}_{\{G_{s,N,t}>\lambda\}}d\left(\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}\mu_{s,R}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\mathds{1}_{\{G_{s,N,t}>\lambda\}}d\left(\Phi_{N}(t)_{\#}\mu_{s,R}\right)=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mu_{s,R}(\Phi_{N}(-t)(G_{s,N,t}>\lambda))\end{split}

Here, we can make use of estimate (
8.8
) and deduce that for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exists
C=C_{s,R,\alpha}>0
such that

\mu_{s,R}(G_{s,N,t}>\lambda)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\mu_{s,R}(G_{s,N,t}>\lambda)^{1-\alpha}\textnormal{exp}\left(C(1+|t|)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\right)

So,

\mu_{s,R}(G_{s,N,t}>\lambda)\leq\lambda^{-1/\alpha}\textnormal{exp}\left(C^{\prime}(1+|t|)^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\right)=:\lambda^{-1/\alpha}C_{s,R,\alpha,t}

(Where
C^{\prime}=\frac{C}{\alpha}
still depends only on
s,R
and
\alpha
). Plugging this into (
8.10
) yields

\left\lVert G_{s,N,t}\right\rVert_{L^{p}(d\mu_{s,R})}^{p}\leq p+\int_{1}^{\infty}\lambda^{p-1}\mu_{s,R}(G_{s,N,t}>\lambda)d\lambda\leq p+C_{s,R,\alpha,t}\int_{1}^{\infty}\lambda^{p-1-1/\alpha}d\lambda

Choosing
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
p-1-\frac{1}{\alpha}<-1
, that is
\alpha<\frac{1}{p}
, we have that the integral
\int_{1}^{\infty}\lambda^{p-1-1/\alpha}d\lambda
is finite. Hence, denoting by

C(s,R,p,t):=p+C_{s,R,\alpha,t}\int_{1}^{\infty}\lambda^{p-1-1/\alpha}d\lambda

leads the result.
∎

Proposition 8.9
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
and
R>0
. Then, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and every
p\in[1,+\infty)
,
G_{s,N}(t,.)
converges to
G_{s}(t,.)
in
\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s,R})
.

Proof.

Let
q\in(1,+\infty)
. From Proposition
8.8
and
6.5
, we know that the two following facts hold:

\begin{cases}\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\left\lVert G_{s,N}(t,.)\right\rVert_{L^{q}(d\mu_{s,R})}<+\infty\\
\text{$G_{s,N}(t,.)$ converges in measure to $G_{s}(t,.)$ (with respect to $\mu_{s,R}$)}\end{cases}

Then – see for example
[
31
]
Remark 3.8 – it implies that for every
p\in[1,q)
,
G_{s,N}(t,.)
converges in
\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s,R})
to
G_{s}(t,.)
. Since
q\in(1,+\infty)
is arbitrary, we deduce that this convergence holds for every
p\in[1,+\infty)
.
∎

We conclude this section by a short remark:

Remark 8.10
.

Starting from Proposition
8.5
, it would have also been possible to prove (with the notations of Proposition
7.6
) that the densities
F_{s,N}(t,.)
,
F_{s}(t,.)
of the transported weighted Gaussian measures belongs to
\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\rho_{s,R})
and that
F_{s,N}(t,.)
converges to
F_{s}(t,.)
in
\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\rho_{s,R})
.

9.
Tools for the energy estimates

In this section, we gather the main tools that we will use in the forthcoming Section
10
,
11
and
12
.

9.1.
Deterministic tools

Notations 9.1

(Number ordering)
.

We will intensively use the following notations:

•

Given a set of frequencies
k_{1},...,k_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
k_{(1)},...,k_{(m)}
a rearrangement of the
k_{j}^{\prime}s
such that

|k_{(1)}|\geq|k_{(2)}|\geq...\geq|k_{(m)}|

•

Similarly, given a set of dyadic integers
N_{1},...,N_{m}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote by
N_{(1)},...,N_{(m)}
a non-decreasing rearrangement of the
N_{j}
.

Examples 9.2
.

For example, we have :

•

If
k_{1}=-1
,
k_{2}=0
,
k_{3}=2
then
k_{(1)}=k_{3}
,
k_{(2)}=k_{1}
, and
k_{(3)}=k_{2}

•

If
N_{1}=8
,
N_{2}=2
,
N_{3}=4
then
N_{(1)}=N_{1}
,
N_{(2)}=N_{3}
, and
N_{(3)}=N_{2}

Lemma 9.3

(Counting bound)
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for every dyadic integers
N_{1},...,N_{m}
, every
\varepsilon_{1},...,\varepsilon_{m}\in\{-1,+1\}
, and every
\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(9.1)

\sum_{k_{1},...,k_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathds{1}_{\varepsilon_{1}k_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}k_{2}+...+\varepsilon_{m}k_{m}=\kappa}\cdot\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}\right)\leq CN_{(2)}N_{(3)}...N_{(m)}

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
N_{j}=N_{(j)}
. The idea of the proof is to let free the variables
k_{2},...,k_{m}
and to freeze the variable
k_{1}
thanks to the constraint
\varepsilon_{1}k_{1}=\kappa-\varepsilon_{2}k_{2}-...-\varepsilon_{m}k_{m}
:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},...,k_{m}\\
|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}_{\varepsilon_{1}k_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}k_{2}+...+\varepsilon_{m}k_{m}=\kappa}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},...,k_{m}\\
|k_{j}|\sim N_{j},j\geq 2\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\sum_{|k_{1}|\sim N_{1}}\mathds{1}_{\varepsilon_{1}k_{1}=\kappa-\varepsilon_{2}k_{2}-...-\varepsilon_{m}k_{m}}}_{\leq 1}\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},...,k_{m}\\
|k_{j}|\sim N_{j},j\geq 2\end{subarray}}1\lesssim N_{2}N_{3}...N_{m}

∎

In the proof of energy estimates, we will use the following estimate on
\psi_{2s}
as a starting point.

Lemma 9.4
.

Set

\displaystyle\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{6}(-1)^{j-1}|k_{j}|^{2s},

\displaystyle\Omega(\vec{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{6}(-1)^{j-1}k_{j}^{2}

There exists a constant
C(s)>0
such that for every
k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k_{4}+k_{5}-k_{6}=0
,

|\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})|\leq C(s)|k_{(1)}|^{2s-2}\left(|\Omega(\vec{k})|+|k_{(3)}|^{2}\right)

Proof.

Essentially, we have to consider two cases : when
k_{(1)}=k_{1}
,
k_{(2)}=k_{2}
and
k_{(1)}=k_{1}
,
k_{(2)}=k_{3}
. In any case, we can assume that
|k_{(3)}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|k_{(2)}|
. Otherwise, using the fact that the constraint
k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k_{4}+k_{5}-k_{6}=0
implies that
|k_{(2)}|\sim|k_{(1)}|
, we would deduce that
|k_{(3)}|\sim|k_{(1)}|
. Therefore, the a priori bound
|\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})|\lesssim|k_{(1)}|^{2s}
would guarantee the desired inequality.

Case 1
: Firstly, we consider the case
k_{(1)}=k_{1}
and
k_{(2)}=k_{2}
. We use the mean value theorem :

\begin{split}|\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})|&\leq k_{1}^{2s}-k_{2}^{2s}+|k_{3}^{2s}-k_{4}^{2s}+k_{5}^{2s}-k_{6}^{2s}|\\
&\leq\sup_{t\in[k_{2}^{2},k_{1}^{2}]}\frac{d}{dt}(t^{s})(k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})+4k_{(3)}^{2s}\\
&\leq s|k_{(1)}|^{2(s-1)}(\Omega(\vec{k})-k_{3}^{2}+k_{4}^{2}-k_{5}^{2}+k_{6}^{2})+4k_{(1)}^{2(s-1)}k_{(3)}^{2}\\
&\leq C(s)|k_{(1)}|^{2(s-1)}(|\Omega(\vec{k})|+|k_{(3)}|^{2})\end{split}

which is the desired inequality.

Case 2
: Secondly, we consider the case
k_{(1)}=k_{1}
and
k_{(2)}=k_{3}
. Since we assume that
k_{(3)}\leq\frac{1}{2}|k_{(2)}|=\frac{1}{2}|k_{3}|
, we have

\Omega(\vec{k})\geq k_{1}^{2}+k_{3}^{2}-|k_{2}^{2}+k_{4}^{2}-k_{5}^{2}+k_{6}^{2}|\geq k_{1}^{2}+k_{3}^{2}-4k_{(3)}^{2}\geq k_{1}^{2}

Therefore, the a priori bound
|\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})|\lesssim|k_{(1)}|^{2s}
guarantees the desired inequality.
∎

In order to make our analysis work for the full range
s>\frac{3}{2}
, we will use the followinw Strichartz estimate for the linear propagator of the Schrödinger equation–see
[
3
]
.

Theorem 9.5

(Strichartz estimate)
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for any function
g
on
\mathbb{T}
:

\left\lVert e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}g\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{6}(\mathbb{T}_{t}\times\mathbb{T}_{x})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{H^{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})}

As a consequence, we can prove the following useful estimate :

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. There exists a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for any dyadic integers
N_{1},...,N_{6}
and any sequences
\left(f_{k_{1}}^{(1)}\right)_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}
,…,
\left(f_{k_{6}}^{(6)}\right)_{k_{6}\in\mathbb{Z}}
of complex numbers that satisfy
f^{(j)}_{k_{j}}=\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}f^{(j)}_{k_{j}}
, we have for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}
:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=\kappa\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|\leq C_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{\varepsilon}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert f^{(j)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}

Proof.

We set

\displaystyle F_{0}^{(1)}(x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}|f_{k_{1}}^{(1)}|e^{ik_{1}x},

\displaystyle F^{(1)}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}e^{it\kappa}F_{0}^{(1)}(x)

and for
j\geq 2
,

\displaystyle F_{0}^{(j)}(x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|e^{ik_{j}x},

\displaystyle F^{(j)}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}F_{0}^{(j)}(x)

Let us now prove the identity :

(9.2)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=\kappa\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{t}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{x}}\left(F^{(1)}\overline{F^{(2)}}F^{(3)}\overline{F^{(4)}}F^{(5)}\overline{F^{(6)}}\right)(t,x)\frac{dxdt}{(2\pi)^{2}}

Firstly, we expand

\left(F^{(1)}\overline{F^{(2)}}F^{(3)}\overline{F^{(4)}}F^{(5)}\overline{F^{(6)}}\right)(t,x)=\sum_{k_{1},...,k_{6}\in\mathbb{Z}}|f_{k_{1}}^{(1)}...f_{k_{6}}^{(6)}|e^{ix(k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6})}e^{-it(\Omega(\vec{k})-\kappa)}

Secondly, we integrate with respect to
x
and
t

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{t}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{x}}F^{(1)}\overline{F^{(2)}}&F^{(3)}\overline{F^{(4)}}F^{(5)}\overline{F^{(6)}}(t,x)\frac{dxdt}{(2\pi)^{2}}\\
&=\sum_{k_{1},...,k_{6}\in\mathbb{Z}}|f_{k_{1}}^{(1)}...f_{k_{6}}^{(6)}|\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}_{t}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{x}}e^{ix(k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6})}e^{-it(\Omega(\vec{k})-\kappa)}\frac{dxdt}{(2\pi)^{2}}\right)\end{split}

Then, the formula (
9.2
) follows from the fact that

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{t}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{x}}e^{ix(k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6})}e^{-it(\Omega(\vec{k})-\kappa)}\frac{dxdt}{(2\pi)^{2}}=\mathds{1}_{k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0}\mathds{1}_{\Omega(\vec{k})=\kappa}

Starting now from (
9.2
) and using the Hölder’s inequality and Theorem
9.5
we get that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=\kappa\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|\leq\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert F^{(j)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{6}(\mathbb{T}_{t}\times\mathbb{T}_{x})}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert F_{0}^{(j)}\right\rVert_{H^{\frac{\varepsilon}{6}}(\mathbb{T}_{x})}

We also used the fact that
\left\lVert F^{(1)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{6}(\mathbb{T}_{t}\times\mathbb{T}_{x})}=\left\lVert e^{it\kappa}e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}F^{(1)}_{0}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{6}(\mathbb{T}_{t}\times\mathbb{T}_{x})}=\left\lVert e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}F^{(1)}_{0}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{6}(\mathbb{T}_{t}\times\mathbb{T}_{x})}
.

On the other hand, with the localizations of the sequences
\left(f^{(j)}_{k_{j}}\right)_{k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}}
, we can write

\left\lVert F_{0}^{(j)}\right\rVert_{H^{\frac{\varepsilon}{6}}(\mathbb{T}_{x})}\lesssim N_{j}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{6}}\left\lVert f^{(j)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{6}}\left\lVert f^{(j)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}

Coming back to the previous estimate we obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=\kappa\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|\leq C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{\varepsilon}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert f^{(j)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}

which is the desired estimate.
∎

Finally, we conclude this section with the following suitable lemma. Although its significance will become apparent later (in the proof of energy estimates in Section
10
,
11
and
12
), we prove it now for a better clarity.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon>0
. There exists a constant
C_{\varepsilon,s}>0
such that for any dyadic integers
N_{1},...,N_{6}
and any sequences
\left(f_{k_{1}}^{(1)}\right)_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}
,…,
\left(f_{k_{6}}^{(6)}\right)_{k_{6}\in\mathbb{Z}}
of complex numbers that satisfy
f^{(j)}_{k_{j}}=\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}f^{(j)}_{k_{j}}
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\right|\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|\leq C_{\varepsilon,s}N_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}N_{(3)}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert f^{(j)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}

Proof.

We start by applying Lemma
9.4
:

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\right|\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|&\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}|k_{(1)}|^{2s-2}\left(1+\frac{|k_{(3)}|^{2}}{|\Omega(\vec{k})|}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|\\
&\lesssim\hskip 14.22636pt\textbf{I}+\textbf{II}\end{split}

where we denote

\textbf{I}:=N_{(1)}^{2s-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|

and,

\textbf{II}:=N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|\Omega(\vec{k})|}\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|

We estimate separately
I
and
II
.

•
Estimate of I :
Removing the constraint
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0
and using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the
k_{(1)},k_{(2)}
summations, we get that

\begin{split}\textbf{I}&\leq N_{(1)}^{2s-2}\left\lVert f^{\left((1)\right)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}\left\lVert f^{\left((2)\right)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}\prod_{j=3}^{6}\left\lVert f^{\left((j)\right)}\right\rVert_{l^{1}}\\
&\leq N_{(1)}^{2s-2}\left(N_{(3)}N_{(4)}N_{(5)}N_{(6)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert f^{(j)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}\\
&\leq N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert f^{(j)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}\end{split}

which is even better than the desired bound.

•
Estimate of II :
Firstly, we observe that
|\Omega(\vec{k})|\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2}
so we can write

\textbf{II}\leq N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}\sum_{N_{(1)}^{2}\gtrsim|\kappa|\geq 1}\frac{1}{|\kappa|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=\kappa\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|

Secondly, we use Lemma
9.6
that says

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=\kappa\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|\leq C_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert f^{(j)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}

Finally, we invoke the well-known estimate

\sum_{N_{(1)}^{2}\gtrsim|\kappa|\geq 1}\frac{1}{|\kappa|}\lesssim\textnormal{log}\left(N_{(1)}\right)\leq C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}

and the combination of those inequalities yields

\textbf{II}\leq C^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}N_{(3)}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert f^{(j)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}

which is the desired bound.
This completes the proof of Lemma
9.7
.
∎

9.2.
Some properties of Gaussian measures

Our analysis for the energy estimates in Section
11
and
12
will also require the following probabilistic tools.

Lemma 9.8

(Moments of Gaussian measures)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
. Then, there exists
C=C(s,\sigma)>0
such that for all
m\geq 1
:

(9.3)

\Big(\int_{H^{\sigma}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{m}d\mu_{s}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\leq Cm^{\frac{1}{2}}

Proof.

From Fernique’s theorem (see for example
[
17
]
,
[
2
]
), there exists
\alpha>0
such that:

\int_{H^{\sigma}}e^{\alpha\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{2}}d\mu_{s}<+\infty

Then, by Markov’s inequality, we obtain the following large deviation estimate:

\mu_{s}(\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}>\lambda)=\mu_{s}(e^{\alpha\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{2}}>e^{\alpha\lambda^{2}})\leq e^{-\alpha\lambda^{2}}\int_{H^{\sigma}}e^{\alpha\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{2}}d\mu_{s}\leq Ce^{-\alpha\lambda^{2}}

Combining this estimate with Cavalieri’s principle, we have:

\int_{H^{\sigma}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{m}d\mu_{s}=m\int_{0}^{+\infty}\lambda^{m-1}\mu_{s}({\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}>\lambda})d\lambda\leq Cm\int_{0}^{+\infty}\lambda^{m-1}e^{-\alpha\lambda^{2}}d\lambda

To conclude, we perform
\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor-1
integration by parts on the integral on the right hand side:

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\lambda^{m-1}e^{-\alpha\lambda^{2}}d\lambda&=\frac{m-2}{2\alpha}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\lambda^{m-3}e^{-\alpha\lambda^{2}}d\lambda\\
&=...=\frac{(m-2)(m-4)...(m-2(\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor-1))}{(2\alpha)^{\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor-1}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\lambda^{m-2(\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor-1)-1}e^{-\alpha\lambda^{2}}d\lambda\\
&\leq\left(\frac{m}{2\alpha}\right)^{\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor-1}\sup_{\widetilde{m}\in[0,2]}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\lambda^{\widetilde{m}}e^{-\alpha\lambda^{2}}d\lambda\end{split}

∎

Next, we state a conditional Wiener chaos estimate which will play a crucial role in the energy estimates. In the sequel, for any complex number
z
, we adopt the notation
z^{+}=z
and
z^{-}=\overline{z}
, called respectively positive and negative
signature
of
z
.

Lemma 9.9

(Conditional Wiener chaos estimate)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathcal{B}
be a
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega
such that
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{A}
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\iota_{1},...,\iota_{m}\in\{-,+\}
. We consider the following expression :

F(\omega):=\sum_{k_{1},...,k_{m}}c_{k_{1},...,k_{m}}(\omega)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{m}g_{k_{j}}^{\iota_{j}}(\omega),\hskip 28.45274pt\omega\in\Omega

where, the
g_{k_{j}}(\omega)
are complex standard i.i.d Gaussians, independent of the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
, and the
c_{k_{1},...,k_{m}}(\omega)
are
\mathcal{B}
-mesurable complex random variables. Then, there exists
C>0
such that for every
p\geq 2
, we have :

\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(\Omega|\mathcal{B})}\leq Cp^{\frac{m}{2}}\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega|\mathcal{B})}

where
\mathbb{L}^{p}(\Omega|\mathcal{B})
is the
\mathbb{L}^{p}
-norm conditioned to the
\sigma
- algebra
\mathcal{B}
.

In the energy estimates, we will apply this lemma with
m=3
,
\mathcal{B}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by low-frequency Gaussians, and the random variables
c_{k_{1},..,k_{m}}
will be some multi-linear expression of high-frequency Gaussians (independent of the low-frequency Gaussians). For a proof of this lemma, we refer to
[
27
]
(see also
[
32
]
and
[
30
]
).

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\iota_{1},...,\iota_{m}\in\{-,+\}
, and
g_{k_{1}},...,g_{k_{m}}
be complex standard i.i.d Gaussians. We consider the following multi-linear expression of Gaussians:

(9.4)

G(\omega):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},...,k_{m}\\
\forall\iota_{i}\neq\iota_{j},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{i}\neq k_{j}\end{subarray}}c_{k_{1},...,k_{m}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{m}g_{k_{j}}^{\iota_{j}}(\omega),\hskip 28.45274pt\omega\in\Omega

where
c_{k_{1},...,k_{m}}
is a sequence of
l^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{m};\mathbb{C})
. Then, there exists
C>0
such that:

(9.5)

\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\biggl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},...,k_{m}\\
\forall\iota_{i}\neq\iota_{j},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{i}\neq k_{j}\end{subarray}}|c_{k_{1},...,k_{m}}|^{2}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{2}}

Proof.

For more readability, we perform the proof only for
m=3
(and that is the value of
m
we will use with this lemma). In addition, we assume that
\iota_{1},\iota_{3}=1
and
\iota_{2}=-1
(the other cases are similar). Thus, we want to prove that:

\big\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\end{subarray}}c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}g_{k_{1}}\overline{g_{k_{2}}}g_{k_{3}}\big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq C\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\end{subarray}}|c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}|^{2}

We start by expanding the left hand side:

(9.6)

\big\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\end{subarray}}c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}g_{k_{1}}\overline{g_{k_{2}}}g_{k_{3}}\big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\hskip 2.84544pt\&\hskip 2.84544ptl_{2}\neq l_{1},l_{3}\end{subarray}}c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\overline{c_{l_{1},l_{2},l_{3}}}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathbb{E}[g_{k_{1}}\overline{g_{k_{2}}}g_{k_{3}}\overline{g_{l_{1}}}g_{l_{2}}\overline{g_{l_{3}}}]

From the independence of the Gaussians
g_{n}
, and the fact that for a complex standard Gaussian variable
g
we have
\mathbb{E}[g]=\mathbb{E}[g^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[g^{3}]=0
, the only non-zero contributions in the sum above is when for each
g_{k_{j}}^{\pm}
there exists an
g_{l_{i}}^{\mp}
, with the opposite signature, such that
k_{j}=l_{i}
. Hence, the only non-zero contributions are of the form:

c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\overline{c_{l_{1},l_{2},l_{3}}}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathbb{E}[|g_{k_{1}}|^{2}|g_{k_{2}}|^{2}|g_{k_{3}}|^{2}]
with
k_{2}=l_{2}
,
\{k_{1},k_{3}\}=\{l_{1},l_{3}\}
and
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}

Then, we invoke the following set of indices:

\begin{split}D_{1}&:=\{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544ptk_{2}=l_{2},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{1}=l_{1},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{3}=l_{3},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{1}\neq k_{3}\}\\
D_{2}&:=\{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544ptk_{2}=l_{2},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{1}=l_{3},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{3}=l_{1},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{1}\neq k_{3}\}\\
D_{3}&:=\{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544ptk_{2}=l_{2},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{1}=k_{3}=l_{1}=l_{3},\hskip 2.84544ptk_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\}\end{split}

so that, coming back to (
9.6
), we have (for any complex standard Gaussian
g
):

\begin{split}\big\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\end{subarray}}c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}g_{k_{1}}\overline{g_{k_{2}}}g_{k_{3}}\big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}&=\mathbb{E}[|g|^{2}]^{3}\sum_{D_{1}}|c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}|^{2}+\mathbb{E}[|g|^{2}]^{3}\sum_{D_{2}}c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\overline{c_{k_{3},k_{2},k_{1}}}\\
&+\mathbb{E}[|g|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|g|^{4}]\sum_{D_{3}}|c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{1}}|^{2}\end{split}

This concludes the proof since:

\sum_{D_{1}}|c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}|^{2}+\sum_{D_{3}}|c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{1}}|^{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\hskip 2.84544pt\&\hskip 2.84544ptk_{1}\neq k_{3}\end{subarray}}|c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}|^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1}\end{subarray}}|c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{1}}|^{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\end{subarray}}|c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}|^{2}

and,

\sum_{D_{2}}c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\overline{c_{k_{3},k_{2},k_{1}}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\hskip 2.84544pt\&\hskip 2.84544ptk_{1}\neq k_{3}\end{subarray}}|c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\hskip 2.84544pt\&\hskip 2.84544ptk_{1}\neq k_{3}\end{subarray}}|c_{k_{3},k_{2},k_{1}}|^{2}\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\\
k_{2}\neq k_{1},k_{3}\end{subarray}}|c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}|^{2}

∎

Remark 9.11
.

When
\iota_{i}\neq\iota_{j}

and

\hskip 2.84544ptk_{i}=k_{j}
, we say that
k_{i}
and
k_{j}
are paired. If one allows such parings in the sum in (
9.4
), then the inequality (
9.5
) does not hold anymore in general. In our analysis in the upcoming sections, we will not encounter such pairings. However, in
[
29
]
, this situation occurred, and the authors needed to study these pairing contributions separately.

10.
Proofs of the deterministic properties

This section is dedicated to the proof of the deterministic properties of the energy correction
R_{s,N}
and of the derivative of the modified energy
Q_{s,N}
. More precisely, we prove here Proposition
3.1
and Proposition
4.1
. To do so, we are going to use the deterministic tools from Section
9
.

Deterministic estimate for the energy correction

In this paragraph, we prove Proposition
3.1
. Recall that we want to estimate the sum of positive terms in (
3.1
).

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Let
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
and let
u^{(1)},...,u^{(6)}\in H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
. First, we decompose the sum in (
3.1
) dyadically:

(10.1)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\big||u^{(1)}_{k_{1}}u^{(2)}_{k_{2}}...u^{(6)}_{k_{6}}|=\sum_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6})

where the summations are performed on the dyadic values of
N_{1},...,N_{6}
and,

\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\big|\prod_{j=1}^{6}\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}|u^{(j)}_{k_{j}}|

Now, using Lemma
9.7
yields:

(10.2)

\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}N_{(3)}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert P_{N_{j}}u^{(j)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}N_{(3)}^{2}(N_{(1)}...N_{(6)})^{-\sigma}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert u^{(j)}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}

where
P_{N}
is the projector onto frequencies
|k|\sim N
. Note that we have
N_{(1)}\sim N_{(2)}
because the constraint
k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0
implies that
|k_{(2)}|\sim|k_{(1)}|
. Besides, we crudely estimate
(N_{(4)}N_{(5)}N_{(3)})^{-\sigma}\lesssim 1
. Then, we rewrite the inequality above as:

(10.3)

\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon}N_{(3)}^{2-\sigma}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert u^{(j)}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}

Next, let us observe that for
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon>-1\\
2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon\longrightarrow-1\hskip 5.69046pt\text{as}\begin{cases}\sigma\rightarrow s-\frac{1}{2}\\
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\end{cases}\end{cases}

and,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2-\sigma>\frac{5}{2}-s\\
2-\sigma\longrightarrow\frac{5}{2}-s\hskip 5.69046pt\text{as}\begin{cases}\sigma\rightarrow s-\frac{1}{2}\\
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\end{cases}\end{cases}

In particular, in (
10.3
),
N_{(1)}
is accompanied by a negative exponent
9
9

9

In dimension
d\geq 2
, we have
\sigma<s-\frac{d}{2}
, and this scenario would be worse because we would have
2(s-1-\sigma)>0
, so
N_{(1)}
would be accompanied by a positive exponent. This scenario is encountered in
[
29
]
, with
d=3
.
.

Finally, if:

\displaystyle-1+(\frac{5}{2}-s)<0,

that is if:

\displaystyle s>\frac{3}{2},

then, for
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
respectively close enough to
s-\frac{1}{2}
and 0, we deduce from (
10.3
) that
10
10

10

The notation
N^{-}
means that the power of
N
is
-\gamma
for a certain
\gamma>0
.
:

\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert u^{(j)}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}

which is summable in the
N_{j}
. Coming back to (
10.1
) finishes the proof.
∎

Deterministic estimate for the derivative of the modified energy

In this paragraph, we prove Proposition
4.1
. Recall that we want to estimate the sums of positive terms in (
4.1
), (
4.2
) and (
4.3
).

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Let
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
.

•
Estimate for
\mathcal{T}_{0}
:
The analysis is similar to the one for
\mathcal{R}
. Indeed, let us fix
u^{(1)},...,u^{(6)}\in H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
. Then, decomposing the sum in (
4.1
) dyadically and using Lemma
9.4
(with
\Omega(\vec{k})=0
), leads to:

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=0\end{subarray}}|\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})||u^{(1)}_{k_{1}}u^{(2)}_{k_{2}}...u^{(6)}_{k_{6}}|&\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=0\end{subarray}}|k_{(1)}|^{2s-2}|k_{(3)}|^{2}|u^{(1)}_{k_{1}}u^{(2)}_{k_{2}}...u^{(6)}_{k_{6}}|\\
&\lesssim\sum_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}\mathfrak{T}_{0}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\end{split}

where,

\mathfrak{T}_{0}(N_{1},...,N_{6}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=0\end{subarray}}N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}|u^{(j)}_{k_{j}}|

Next, from Lemma
9.6
(with
f^{j}_{k_{j}}=\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}|u^{(j)}_{k_{j}}|
), we have:

\mathfrak{T}_{0}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}N_{(3)}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert P_{N_{j}}u^{(j)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

From that point the proof goes the same as the one for
\mathcal{R}
(see the estimate (
10.2
)).

•
Estimate of
\mathcal{T}_{j}
for
j=1,2
:
We only prove the estimate (
4.2
) since the analysis for the estimate (
4.3
) is similar. Here, the computations are a little more delicate. For the sake of readability, we restrict ourselves to prove the estimate:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\big||u_{p_{1}}\overline{u_{p_{2}}}...u_{p_{5}}\overline{u_{k_{2}}}...\overline{u_{k_{6}}}|\leq C_{s}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{10}

In other words, we just prove the estimate (
4.2
) where all the
u^{(j)}
and
v^{(l)}
are equal to a single
u
.
To do so, we will use the following lemma:

Lemma 10.1
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for any dyadic integers
P_{1},...,P_{5}
and any sequences
\left(g^{P_{1}}_{p_{1}}\right)_{p_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}},...,\left(g^{P_{5}}_{p_{5}}\right)_{p_{5}\in\mathbb{Z}}
of complex numbers that satisfy for
j=1,...,5
,
g^{P_{j}}_{p_{j}}=\mathds{1}_{|p_{j}|\sim P_{j}}g_{p_{j}}
, if we set for
k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
:

f_{k_{1}}:=\sum_{p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}}g_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}g_{p_{2}}^{P_{2}}g_{p_{3}}^{P_{3}}g_{p_{4}}^{P_{4}}g_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}

Then,

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}\leq C\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{5}\left\lVert g^{P_{j}}\right\rVert_{l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}

For clarity, we postpone the proof of this lemma for the end of this section. Now we are ready to prove the estimate. Once again, we start by decomposing the sum as:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\big||u_{p_{1}}\overline{u_{p_{2}}}...u_{p_{5}}\overline{u_{k_{2}}}...\overline{u_{k_{6}}}|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M_{1},...,M_{6}\\
P_{1},...,P_{5}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5})

where
M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}
are dyadic-valued and

(10.4)

\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\big||u_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}...u_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}u_{k_{2}}^{M_{2}}...u_{k_{6}}^{M_{6}}|\cdot\mathds{1}_{|k_{1}|\sim M_{1}}

with the practical notation
u_{l}^{N}:=\mathds{1}_{|l|\sim N}u_{l}
. Next, we rewrite
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
as:

(10.5)

\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,P_{5})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\big|\prod_{j=1}^{6}|f_{k_{j}}^{(j)}|

where, for
j=2,...,6
we denote
f_{k_{j}}^{M_{j}}:=u_{k_{j}}^{M_{j}}
, and:

f_{k_{1}}^{(1)}:=\mathds{1}_{|k_{1}|\sim M_{1}}\cdot\sum_{p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}}|u_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}...u_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}|

At this stage, let us recall that
M_{(1)}\geq...\geq M_{(6)}
and
P_{(1)}\geq...\geq P_{(5)}
are respectively a non-increasing rearrangement of
M_{1},...,M_{6}
and
P_{1},...,P_{5}
. We also introduce:

N_{(1)}\geq N_{(2)}\geq...\geq N_{(10)}
a non-increasing rearrangement of
M_{2},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}

Note that the constraints in the sum in
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
imply that

M_{(1)}\sim M_{(2)}
, and:
P_{(1)}\sim P_{(2)}
or
P_{(1)}\sim M_{1}
, and:
N_{(1)}\sim N_{(2)}
.

Now, we use Lemma
9.7
to estimate
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
in (
10.5
); we obtain that for any
\varepsilon>0
:

\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}M_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}M_{(3)}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert f^{(j)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}M_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}M_{(3)}^{2}\left\lVert f^{(1)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}\prod_{j=2}^{6}\left\lVert u^{M_{j}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

Applying the well-suited estimate of
\left\lVert f^{(1)}\right\rVert_{l^{2}}
from Lemma
10.1
leads to

(10.6)

\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}M_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}M_{(3)}^{2}(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)})^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{5}\left\lVert u^{P_{j}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\prod_{j=2}^{6}\left\lVert u^{M_{j}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

and it follows that

\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}M_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}M_{(3)}^{2}\left(M_{2}M_{3}M_{4}M_{5}M_{6}\right)^{-\sigma}P_{(1)}^{-\sigma}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{10}

In the remaining part of the proof, we show that it implies that:

(10.7)

\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{-}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{10}

for
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
close enough to
s-\frac{1}{2}
, which will complete the estimate for
\mathcal{T}_{1}
. We start from the inequality above and we use the fact that
M_{1}\lesssim P_{(1)}
due to the constraint
p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}
, along with the rough estimates
(M_{(4)}M_{(5)}M_{(6)})^{-\sigma}\leq 1
and
(P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)})^{\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}\leq 1
, in order to obtain:

(10.8)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,P_{5})&\lesssim_{\varepsilon}M_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}M_{4}M_{5}M_{6})^{-\sigma}(M_{1}^{\sigma}P_{(1)}^{-\sigma})(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)})^{\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{10}\\
&\lesssim_{\varepsilon}M_{(1)}^{2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon}M_{(3)}^{2-\sigma}P_{(2)}^{\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{10}\end{split}

Besides, when
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
are respectively arbitrarily close to
s-\frac{1}{2}
and
0
, we have that
2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon
,
2-\sigma
and
\frac{1}{2}-\sigma
are respectively arbitrarily close to

\displaystyle 2(s-1-(s-\frac{1}{2}))

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle 2-(s-\frac{1}{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{2}-s,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}-(s-\frac{1}{2})=1-s

Hence,
M_{(1)}
is accompanied by a negative exponent,
P_{(2)}
is accompanied by a negative exponent (since
s>\frac{3}{2}>1
), and
M_{(3)}
is accompanied by a negative exponent when
s\geq\frac{5}{2}
and by a non-negative one when
s<\frac{5}{2}
.

–In the situation where
M_{(1)}\sim N_{(1)}
, we use the rough estimate
P_{(2)}^{\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}\leq 1
in (
10.8
), and we obtain:

\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon}M_{(3)}^{2-\sigma}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{10}

which is conclusive as far as

2(s-1-(s-\frac{1}{2}))+2-(s-\frac{1}{2})<0

that is, as far as
s>\frac{3}{2}
.

–In the other situation, where
M_{(1)}\ll N_{(1)}
, we necessarily have
P_{(1)}\sim P_{(2)}\sim N_{(1)}
, so we deduce from (
10.8
) that:

\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}M_{(3)}^{2(s-1-\sigma)+2-\sigma+\varepsilon}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{10}

Then, for
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
respectively arbitrarily close to
s-\frac{1}{2}
and
0
, we have:

\mathfrak{T}_{1}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\lesssim N_{(1)}^{(1-s)+}M_{(3)}^{(\frac{3}{2}-s)+}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{10}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{10}

since
s>\frac{3}{2}
.

In conclusion, (
10.7
) is proven and the proof of Proposition
4.1
is completed.
∎

We finish this section by a proof of Lemma
10.1
.

Proof of Lemma
10.1
.

We have to estimate :

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}^{2}=\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigg|\sum_{p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}}g_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}g_{p_{2}}^{P_{2}}g_{p_{3}}^{P_{3}}g_{p_{4}}^{P_{4}}g_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}\bigg|^{2}

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we obtain:

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}^{2}\leq\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigl(\sum_{p_{1},...,p_{5}}\mathds{1}_{p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}}\prod_{j=1}^{5}\mathds{1}_{|p_{j}|\sim P_{j}}\bigr)\bigl(\sum_{p_{1},...,p_{5}}\mathds{1}_{p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}}|g_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}g_{p_{2}}^{P_{2}}g_{p_{3}}^{P_{3}}g_{p_{4}}^{P_{4}}g_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}|^{2}\bigr)

Plugging the counting bound (
9.1
) into this formula yields

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}^{2}\lesssim P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\sum_{p_{1},...,p_{5}}|g_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}g_{p_{2}}^{P_{2}}g_{p_{3}}^{P_{3}}g_{p_{4}}^{P_{4}}g_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}|^{2}\underbrace{\big(\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathds{1}_{p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}}\big)}_{\leq 1}

Thus,

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}^{2}\lesssim P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\prod_{j=1}^{5}\left\lVert g^{P_{j}}\right\rVert_{l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}^{2}

which is the desired estimate.
∎

11.
Estimates for the weight of the weighted Gaussian measures

This section is dedicated to estimates on the weight
\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}
of the weighted Gaussian measure
\rho_{s,R,N}
(defined in (
7.1
)). In particular, we show that :

\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}\in\mathbb{L}^{1}(d\mu_{s})

which ensures that
\rho_{s,R,N}
is a finite measure on
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
,
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
.
More precisely, we prove Proposition
7.4
. Yet, before doing so, we will need the two following lemmas :

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
. Then, there exists
\beta\in(0,1)
such that for every
R>0
, there exists
C(s,R)>0
, such that for any
p\in[2,+\infty)
,

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}R_{s,N}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\leq C(s,R)p^{\beta}

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Recall that we denote
R_{s,\infty}=R_{s}
for convenience.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{A},d\nu)
be a probability space and let
F:X\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a mesurable function. Assume that there exist constants
C_{0}>0
and
\beta\in(0,1)
such that for every
p\in[2,+\infty)
,

\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\nu)}\leq C_{0}p^{\beta}

Then, there exist
\delta>0
and
C_{1}>0
only depending on
\beta
and
C_{0}
such that :

\int_{X}e^{\delta|F(v)|^{\frac{1}{\beta}}}d\nu(v)\leq C_{1}

We will only prove Lemma
11.1
, Lemma
11.2
being just a slightly different version of Lemma 4.5 from
[
32
]
(where a proof is given).

Before doing the proof of Lemma
11.1
, let us briefly show how it implies Proposition
7.4
when combining with Lemma
11.2
.

Proof of Proposition
7.4
.

Here, we assume the statements in Lemma
11.1
and Lemma
11.2
.

–We start by proving the first statement in Proposition
7.4
. Lemma
11.1
shows that
F:=\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}R_{s,N}(u):H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
11.2
(with
\nu=\mu_{s}
). Then, applying Lemma
11.2
, we obtain that there exist
\delta=\delta(s,R)>0
and
C_{1}(s,R)>0
such that :

\int_{H^{\sigma}}e^{\delta\left|\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}R_{s,N}(u)\right|^{\frac{1}{\beta}}}d\mu_{s}\leq C_{1}(s,R)

A fortiori we have,

\int_{H^{\sigma}}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{\delta\left|R_{s,N}(u)\right|^{\frac{1}{\beta}}}d\mu_{s}\leq C_{1}(s,R)

Thanks to the fact that
\frac{1}{\beta}>1
, we deduce from the above inequality that for every
p\in[1,+\infty)
:

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{|R_{s,N}(u)|}\right\rVert^{p}_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}=\int_{H^{\sigma}}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{p|R_{s,N}(u)|}d\mu_{s}\leq C(s,R,p)<+\infty

for a certain constant
C(s,R,p)>0
.

–As a consequence, we are now able to prove the second statement of Proposition
7.4
, that is:

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s}(u)}-\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Firstly, we know from Proposition
3.3
that we have the pointwise convergence:

\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}R_{s}(u)=\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\cdot\frac{1}{6}\textnormal{Re}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}u_{k_{1}}\overline{u_{k_{2}}}...\overline{u_{k_{6}}}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}R_{s,N}(u)

Consequently, from the continuity of the exponential, we also have the pointwise convergence:

\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s}(u)}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}

In particular,
\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}
converges to
\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s}(u)}
in measure. In addition, for a fixed
q\in(1,+\infty)
, we just proved that the functions
\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}
are uniformly bounded in
\mathbb{L}^{q}(d\mu_{s})
(with respect to
N\in\mathbb{N}
). Then, (using the same argument as the one from the proof of Proposition
8.9
) we can conclude that
\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s,N}(u)}
converges to
\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}e^{-R_{s}(u)}
in
\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})
for any
p\in[1,q)
; and since
q\in(1,+\infty)
is arbitrary, the convergence holds for any
p\in[1,+\infty)
. This completes the proof of Proposition
7.4
.
∎

However, it remains to prove Lemma
11.1
. Our analysis will rely on a decomposition into two parts of
R_{s,N}
. We will be able to treat the first part deterministically thanks to suitable ”exchanges of derivatives”. For the second part, those ”exchanges of derivatives” will fail because we will be in a
high-high-high-low-low-low
regime of frequency (where the three highest frequencies in the sum (
3.2
) defining
R_{s,N}
are in fact much more higher than the three lowest frequencies). Instead, we will handle the second part using the independence between the high frequency Gaussians and the low frequency Gaussians, using Wiener chaos estimate.

11.1.
Decomposition

Recall that we have (see Section
3
, Proposition
3.1
):

R_{s,N}(u)=\frac{1}{6}\textnormal{Re}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathcal{R}(w)

where
w:=\Pi_{N}u
(with the convention
\Pi_{\infty}=id
), and

\mathcal{R}(w):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{k_{1}}\overline{w_{k_{2}}}...\overline{w_{k_{6}}}

It suffices to show the estimate of Lemma
11.1
for
\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{R}(w)
because
|R_{s,N}(u)|\leq\frac{1}{6}|\mathcal{R}(w)|
.

Next, we split the set of indices over which we sum. We invoke the following sets of indices :

(11.1)

\begin{split}\Lambda_{D}:=\{(k_{1},...,k_{6})\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}:\hskip 2.84544pt&\sum_{j=1}^{6}(-1)^{j-1}k_{j}=0,\hskip 2.84544pt\sum_{j=1}^{6}(-1)^{j-1}k_{j}^{2}\neq 0,\\
&\hskip 2.84544pt|k_{(3)}|<|k_{(1)}|^{1-\delta_{0}}\hskip 2.84544pt\textbf{or}\hskip 2.84544pt|k_{(4)}|\geq|k_{(3)}|^{\delta_{0}}\}\end{split}

and,

(11.2)

\begin{split}\Lambda_{W}:=\{(k_{1},...,k_{6})\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}:\hskip 2.84544pt&\sum_{j=1}^{6}(-1)^{j-1}k_{j}=0,\hskip 2.84544pt\sum_{j=1}^{6}(-1)^{j-1}k_{j}^{2}\neq 0,\\
&\hskip 2.84544pt|k_{(3)}|\geq|k_{(1)}|^{1-\delta_{0}}\hskip 2.84544pt\textbf{and}\hskip 2.84544pt|k_{(4)}|<|k_{(3)}|^{\delta_{0}}\}\end{split}

where
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
11
11

11

We will see in the forthcoming proof that there is no constraint on
\delta_{0}
, so we can chose it as any number in
(0,1)
. Then, we split
\mathcal{R}
as :

\mathcal{R}(w)=\mathcal{R}^{(D)}(w)+\mathcal{R}^{(W)}(w)

where,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(D)}(w)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\Lambda_{D}}\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{k_{1}}\overline{w_{k_{2}}}...\overline{w_{k_{6}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(W)}(w):=\sum_{\Lambda_{W}}\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{k_{1}}\overline{w_{k_{2}}}...\overline{w_{k_{6}}}

To estimate
\mathcal{R}^{(D)}
we will use the deterministic tools from Section
9
. To estimate
\mathcal{R}^{(W)}
, we note that in the sum we are in a
high-high-high-low-low-low
regime because
|k_{(1)}|,|k_{(2)}|,|k_{(3)}|\gg|k_{(4)}|,|k_{(5)}|,|k_{(6)}|
. It will then be possible to make use of the independence between the Gaussians
g_{k_{(1)}},g_{k_{(2)}},g_{k_{(3)}}
and
g_{k_{(4)}},g_{k_{(5)}},g_{k_{(6)}}
via Wiener chaos.

11.1.1.
Absence of pairing:

Definition 11.3

(Pairing)
.

Consider a constraint under the form :

\varepsilon_{1}k_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}k_{2}+...+\varepsilon_{m}k_{m}=0

where
k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\varepsilon_{j}\in\{-,+\}
. We say that
k_{j}
and
k_{l}
are paired if

\varepsilon_{j}k_{j}+\varepsilon_{l}k_{l}=0

Remark 11.4
.

In
\Lambda_{W}
(see (
11.2
) above), there is (for
k_{(1)}
large enough) no pairing within the three highest frequencies (relatively to the constraint
k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0
). Indeed, suppose there is a pairing between two of the three highest frequencies. Then, the constraint would take the form:

the remaining high frequency = sum of three low frequencies

which is impossible because in
\Lambda_{W}
we have:

|
high frequencies
|

\gg

|
low frequencies
|

11.2.
Proof of the estimate

We are now ready to prove Lemma
11.1
.

Proof of Lemma
11.1
.

We organize the proof in two steps. In the first step, we provide a (deterministic) estimate for
\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{R}
. In the second step, we show that this estimate is only conclusive for the contribution
\mathcal{R}^{(D)}
, and that for the remaining contribution
\mathcal{R}^{(W)}
, further analysis using Wiener chaos is required.

Step 1, Deterministic estimate :
We rely on the estimate (
10.2
) we obtained in Section
10
(which we do not reprove here). Thus, we start our analysis from the following estimates:

(11.3)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{R}(w)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6})

and:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6})

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}N_{(3)}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert P_{N_{j}}w\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

where
N_{1},...,N_{6}
are dyadic valued,
P_{N}
is the projector onto frequencies
|k|\sim N
, and:

\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\Big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\Big|\prod_{j=1}^{6}\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}|w_{k_{j}}|

We decompose
\mathfrak{R}
as
\mathfrak{R}=\mathfrak{R}^{(D)}+\mathfrak{R}^{(W)}
, according to the decomposition of
\mathcal{R}
.

Now, let
1<\sigma^{\prime}<\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
. We have:

(11.4)

\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert P_{N_{j}}w\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert P_{N_{j}}u\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\lesssim(N_{(1)}^{-\sigma}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}})(N_{(2)}^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma^{\prime}}})(N_{(3)}N_{(4)}N_{(5)}N_{(6)})^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{4}

Recall that the constraints in the sum in
\mathfrak{R}
above implies that
N_{(1)}\sim N_{(2)}
. Moreover, if we write:

\sigma^{\prime}=\alpha 1+(1-\alpha)\sigma

for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, then, by interpolating
H^{\sigma^{\prime}}
between
H^{1}
and
H^{\sigma}
, we obtain from (
11.4
) that:

\begin{split}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert P_{N_{j}}u\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}&\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-2\sigma+\alpha(\sigma-1)}(N_{(3)}N_{(4)}N_{(5)}N_{(6)})^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{4+\alpha}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}}\\
&\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-2\sigma+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}(N_{(3)}N_{(4)}N_{(5)}N_{(6)})^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{4+\alpha}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}},\hskip 8.5359pt(\textnormal{because $\alpha(\sigma-1)<\alpha(s-3/2)$ })\end{split}

Plugging this into (
11.3
), we obtain

\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}N_{(3)}(N_{(4)}N_{(5)}N_{(6)})^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{4+\alpha}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}}

In particular, thanks to Remark
7.2
,

\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}N_{(3)}(N_{(4)}N_{(5)}N_{(6)})^{-1}R^{4+\alpha}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}}

And since we will not need the smallness provided by
(N_{(5)}N_{(6)})^{-1}
, we simply write :

\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\lesssim_{\varepsilon,R}N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}N_{(3)}N_{(4)}^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}}

Using now (
9.3
), we conclude that

(11.5)

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathfrak{R}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\leq CN_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}N_{(3)}N_{(4)}^{-1}p^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}

Where the constant
C>0
depends on
R
,
s
,
\sigma
and
\varepsilon
.
This is the estimate we will work with later in the proof. We stress the fact that (
11.5
) is true for
\mathfrak{R}
and also for
\mathfrak{R}^{(D)}
and
\mathfrak{R}^{(W)}
(with the exact same proof).

Step 2, Estimates for
\mathcal{R}^{(D)}
and
\mathcal{R}^{(W)}
:

•We start with
\mathcal{R}^{(D)}
. Recalling that
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
, we see that the exponent of
N_{(1)}
in (
11.5
) satisfies:

(11.6)

\begin{cases}2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})>-1\\
2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\longrightarrow-1\hskip 5.69046pt\text{as}\begin{cases}\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\\
\alpha\rightarrow 0\\
\sigma\rightarrow s-\frac{1}{2}\end{cases}\end{cases}

Moreover, for a fixed
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
, the conditions on the indices lying in
\Lambda_{D}
imply that
N_{(3)}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{1-\delta_{0}}
or
N_{(4)}\gtrsim N_{(3)}^{\delta_{0}}
. In both cases, we deduce from (
11.5
) that for
\varepsilon
and
\alpha
close enough to
0
and
\sigma
close enough to
s-\frac{1}{2}
, we have
12
12

12

The notation
N^{-}
means that the power of
N
is
-\gamma
for a certain
\gamma>0
.

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathfrak{R}^{(D)}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\leq CN_{(1)}^{-}p^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}

Consequently,

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{R}^{(D)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\leq Cp^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}

•We continue our analysis with the term
\mathcal{R}^{(W)}
. Once again, we start by decomposing
\mathcal{R}^{(W)}
dyadically as
\mathcal{R}^{(W)}(w)=\sum_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}(w)
, where
13
13

13

For the analysis below, we need to keep the complex conjugation bars, so here we don’t use
\mathfrak{R}^{(W)}
:

\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}(w):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\\
|k_{(4)}|\leq|k_{(3)}|^{\delta_{0}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{k_{1}}\overline{w_{k_{2}}}...\overline{w_{k_{6}}}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}

Henceforth, we denote
w_{k_{j}}^{N_{j}}:=\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}w_{k_{j}}
for better readability.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\{N_{1},N_{2},N_{3}\}=\{N_{(1)},N_{(2)},N_{(3)}\}
, meaning that the three highest frequencies are
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
. The other cases are similar or simpler.

We denote
\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the Gaussians
\left(g_{k}\right)_{|k|\leq N_{(3)}/100}
. We only need to consider the contribution when
N_{(1)}
is large because when
N_{(1)}
is small, all the
N_{j}
’s are small, and we can use (
11.5
) without fearing issues of summability in the
N_{j}
’s. In particular, we assume that
N_{(1)}
is sufficiently large so that
N_{(3)}
(which is
\gtrsim N_{(1)}^{1-\delta_{0}}
) is large enough to ensure that
N_{(4)}
(which is
\lesssim N_{(3)}^{\delta_{0}}
) is
\leq N_{(3)}/100
. As a consequence, we have that the random variables :

\begin{split}&w_{k_{4}}^{N_{4}},w_{k_{4}}^{N_{4}},w_{k_{5}}^{N_{6}}\hskip 5.69046pt\textnormal{are $\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}}$mesurable}\\
&w_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}},w_{k_{2}}^{N_{2}},w_{k_{3}}^{N_{3}}\hskip 5.69046pt\textnormal{are independent of $\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}}$}\end{split}

With this set up, we can now begin the analysis. Thanks to Remark
7.2
, we have

\begin{split}\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}&\leq\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{B_{R}^{H^{1}}}(u)\cdot\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\\
&\leq\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{B_{R}^{H^{1}}}(P_{N_{(3)/100}}u)\cdot\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\end{split}

And,
\mathds{1}_{B_{R}^{H^{1}}}(P_{N_{(3)/100}}u)
is
\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}}
mesurable. So, using the
\mathbb{L}^{p}
-norm conditioned to the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}}
, denoted
\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s}|\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}})
, we obtain :

(11.7)

\begin{split}\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}&\leq\Big\|\big\|\mathds{1}_{B_{R}^{H^{1}}}(P_{N_{(3)/100}}u)\cdot\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s}|\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}})}\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(d\mu_{s})}\\
&\leq\Big\|\big\|\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s}|\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}})}\cdot\mathds{1}_{B_{R}^{H^{1}}}(P_{N_{(3)/100}}u)\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(d\mu_{s})}\end{split}

Now, the conditional Wiener-chaos estimate from Lemma
9.9
(with
m=3
), followed by Lemma
9.10
combined with the absence of paring (see Remark
11.4
), allows us to obtain :

(11.8)

\begin{split}\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s}|\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}})}&\lesssim p^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(d\mu_{s}|\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}})}\\
&\lesssim p^{\frac{3}{2}}(N_{(1)}N_{(2)}N_{(3)})^{-s}\bigg(\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\Big|\sum_{k_{4},k_{5},k_{6}}C(\vec{k})\cdot\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\overline{w_{k_{4}}^{N_{4}}}w_{k_{5}}^{N_{5}}\overline{w_{k_{6}}^{N_{6}}}\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\end{split}

where we gathered all the constraints into the term :

C(\vec{k}):=\mathds{1}_{k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0}\cdot\mathds{1}_{\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{6}\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}

Next, by Cauchy-Schwarz,

\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\Big|\sum_{k_{4},k_{5},k_{6}}C(\vec{k})\cdot\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\overline{w_{k_{4}}^{N_{4}}}w_{k_{5}}^{N_{5}}\overline{w_{k_{6}}^{N_{6}}}\Big|^{2}\lesssim\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\Big(\sum_{k_{4},k_{5},k_{6}}C(\vec{k})\cdot\Big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\Big|^{2}|w_{k_{4}}^{N_{4}}w_{k_{5}}^{N_{5}}|^{2}\Big)\Big(\sum_{k_{4},k_{5},k_{6}}C(\vec{k})|w_{k_{6}}^{N_{6}}|^{2}\Big)

From Lemma
9.4
we get
\left|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\right|\lesssim|k_{(1)}|^{2s-2}(1+\frac{|k_{(3)}|^{2}}{|\Omega(\vec{k})|})\lesssim|k_{(1)}|^{2s-2}|k_{(3)}|^{2}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}
. Then, on the one hand :

\begin{split}\sum_{k_{4},k_{5},k_{6}}C(\vec{k})\cdot\Big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\Big|^{2}|w_{k_{4}}^{N_{4}}w_{k_{5}}^{N_{5}}|^{2}&\lesssim(N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2})^{2}\sum_{k_{4},k_{5},k_{6}}C(\vec{k})\cdot|w_{k_{4}}^{N_{4}}w_{k_{5}}^{N_{5}}|^{2}\\
&\lesssim(N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2})^{2}\sum_{k_{4},k_{5}}|w_{k_{4}}^{N_{4}}w_{k_{5}}^{N_{5}}|^{2}\cdot\underbrace{\sum_{k_{6}}\mathds{1}_{k_{6}=k_{1}-k_{2}...+k_{5}}}_{\leq 1}\\
&\lesssim(N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}\left\lVert w^{N_{4}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\lVert w^{N_{5}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}})^{2}\end{split}

and on the other hand,

\begin{split}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\Big(\sum_{k_{4},k_{5},k_{6}}C(\vec{k})|w_{k_{6}}^{N_{6}}|^{2}\Big)=\sum_{k_{6}}|w_{k_{6}}^{N_{6}}|^{2}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},k_{5}}C(\vec{k})\lesssim N_{(2)}N_{(3)}N_{(4)}N_{(5)}\cdot\left\lVert w^{N_{6}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\end{split}

where we used the counting bound from Lemma
9.3
. We deduce from the two inequalities above that

\begin{split}\bigg(\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\Big|\sum_{k_{4},k_{5},k_{6}}C(\vec{k})\cdot\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\overline{w_{k_{4}}^{N_{4}}}w_{k_{5}}^{N_{5}}\overline{w_{k_{6}}^{N_{6}}}\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}&\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}N_{(1)}^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{(3)}^{\frac{1}{2}}(N_{(4)}N_{(5})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert w^{N_{4}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\lVert w^{N_{5}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\lVert w^{N_{6}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\\
&\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2s-2+\frac{1}{2}}N_{(3)}^{\frac{5}{2}}(N_{(4)}N_{(5})^{-\frac{1}{2}}N_{(6)}^{-1}\left\lVert w^{N_{4}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{N_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{N_{6}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\\
&\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2s-\frac{3}{2}}N_{(3)}^{\frac{5}{2}}\left\lVert w^{N_{4}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{N_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{N_{6}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\end{split}

Coming back to (
11.8
), we deduce that

\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s}|\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}})}\lesssim p^{\frac{3}{2}}N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}}N_{(3)}^{\frac{5}{2}-s}\left\lVert w^{N_{4}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{N_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{N_{6}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}

And plugging this into (
11.7
), we obtain:

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\lesssim_{R}p^{\frac{3}{2}}N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}}N_{(3)}^{\frac{5}{2}-s}

Interpolating the above inequality with (
11.5
) (more precisely (
11.5
) with
\mathfrak{R}^{(W)}
instead of
\mathfrak{R}
), we have that for any
\theta\in(0,1)
:

(11.9)

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{R}_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\lesssim p^{\frac{3}{2}\theta+\frac{2-\alpha}{2}(1-\theta)}N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}\theta+\left(2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\right)(1-\theta)}N_{(3)}^{(\frac{5}{2}-s)\theta+(1-\theta)}

Finally, we use the following lemma to finish the proof of Lemma
11.1
:

Lemma 11.5
.

There exist
\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
close enough to
s-\frac{1}{2}
,
\varepsilon>0
close enough to
0
,
\theta\in(0,1)
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, such that

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\theta+\frac{2-\alpha}{2}(1-\theta)

\displaystyle<1,

\displaystyle N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}\theta+\left(2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\right)(1-\theta)}N_{(3)}^{(\frac{5}{2}-s)\theta+(1-\theta)}

\displaystyle\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-}

Let us provide a proof of this lemma:

Proof of Lemma
11.5
.

Firstly, we have:

(11.10)

\begin{split}\frac{3}{2}\theta+\frac{2-\alpha}{2}(1-\theta)<1&\iff\theta\left(\frac{1+\alpha}{2}\right)<1+\frac{\alpha-2}{2}=\frac{\alpha}{2}\\
&\iff\theta<\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}\end{split}

Secondly, regarding the exponent of
N_{(3)}
, we have:

(11.11)

(\frac{5}{2}-s)\theta+(1-\theta)\geq 0\iff\theta\leq\frac{1}{s-\frac{3}{2}}

Let us take
\theta\leq\frac{1}{s-\frac{3}{2}}
(always true when
s\leq\frac{5}{2}
). Then,

N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}\theta+\left(2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\right)(1-\theta)}N_{(3)}^{(\frac{5}{2}-s)\theta+(1-\theta)}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}\theta+\left(2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\right)(1-\theta)}N_{(1)}^{(\frac{5}{2}-s)\theta+(1-\theta)}

We want to have appropriate parameters such that the conditions on
\theta
given in (
11.10
) and (
11.11
) are satisfied along with the following one:

-\frac{3}{2}\theta+\left(2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\right)(1-\theta)+(\frac{5}{2}-s)\theta+(1-\theta)<0

Since
2(s-1-\sigma)+\varepsilon\longrightarrow-1
as
\sigma\rightarrow s-\frac{1}{2}
and
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, the previous condition will be true for
\sigma
and
\varepsilon
respectively close enough to
s-\frac{3}{2}
and
0
if:

-\frac{3}{2}\theta+\left(-1+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\right)(1-\theta)+(\frac{5}{2}-s)\theta+(1-\theta)<0

And this condition is equivalent to the following one:

(11.12)

\begin{split}&\theta\left(1-s-\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\right)<-\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\\
\iff&\theta>\frac{\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}{s-1+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}=\frac{\alpha}{\frac{s-1}{s-\frac{3}{2}}+\alpha}=\frac{\alpha}{1+\frac{1}{2(s-\frac{3}{2})}+\alpha}\end{split}

To conclude, if we first take
\alpha
small enough such that
\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}<\frac{1}{s-\frac{3}{2}}
, and then
\theta
such that

\frac{\alpha}{1+\frac{1}{2(s-3/2)}+\alpha}<\theta<\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha},

the conditions given in (
11.10
), (
11.11
) and (
11.12
) are satisfied. We illustrate this with the following drawing :

0
\frac{1}{s-3/2}
\frac{\alpha}{1+\frac{1}{2(s-3/2)}+\alpha}
\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}
\theta

With those parameters, Lemma
11.5
is proven.
∎

Hence, coming back to (
11.9
), the proof of Lemma
11.1
is completed.
∎

12.
Estimates for the modified energy derivative at 0

This section is dedicated to
L^{p}(d\mu_{s})
estimates on
Q_{s,N}
(defined in (
4.4
), see also Definition
2.3
). More precisely, we prove Proposition
8.2
. The strategy of our proof is the same as the one of Lemma
11.1
. We first obtain a deterministic estimate that will be conclusive except for a frequency regime
high-high-high-low-…-low
(where the three highest frequencies are in fact much more higher than the others). This will lead us to decompose
Q_{s,N}
into two parts, one that captures the
high-high-high-low-…-low
regime, and one that captures the other regime of frequencies. We will handle the
high-high-high-low-…-low
regime using the independence of Gaussians via Wiener-chaos estimate. It will be slightly more complicated than the proof of Lemma
11.1
because of the presence of more indices. However, in our situation, we will not encounter the possibility of a ”pairing between generations”
14
14

14

For example, considering the constraint
k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\hskip 2.84544pt\&\hskip 2.84544ptp_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}
, then according to Definition
11.3
, a ”pairing between generations” corresponds to the situation when one of the
k_{j}
is paired with one of the
p_{l}
.
which could have required the ”remarkable cancellation” that has been presented in
[
29
]
(sections 5 and 7). The reason why we do not encounter such a pairing stems from the fact that we perform the Wiener chaos estimate with respect to three high-frequency Gaussians, that is with
m=3
in Lemma
9.9
. In doing so, we prevent a pairing between generations from occurring (see Paragraph
12.1.1
). In
[
29
]
, the Wiener chaos estimates are performed with respect to two high-frequency Gaussians, that is with
m=2
in Lemma
9.9
, and in this situation, a pairing between generation may occur.

Preliminaries

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
Recall that from (
4.4
) we have:

Q_{s,N}(u)=Q_{s,N}(w)=\textnormal{Im}(-\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{Q}_{0}(w)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}_{1}(w)-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}_{2}(w))

where
w:=\Pi_{N}u
(with the convention
\Pi_{\infty}=id
), and:

\mathcal{Q}_{0}(w):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=0\end{subarray}}\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})w_{k_{1}}\overline{w_{k_{2}}}...\overline{w_{k_{6}}}

and,

\mathcal{Q}_{1}(w):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{p_{1}}\overline{w_{p_{2}}}...w_{p_{5}}\overline{w_{k_{2}}}...\overline{w_{k_{6}}}

and,

\mathcal{Q}_{2}(w):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
q_{1}-q_{2}+...+q_{5}=k_{2}\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{k_{1}}\overline{w_{q_{1}}}w_{q_{2}}...\overline{w_{q_{5}}}w_{k_{3}}...\overline{w_{k_{6}}}
