It suffices to show the estimates for
\mathcal{Q}_{0},\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
because
|Q_{s,N}(u)|\leq|\mathcal{Q}_{0}(w)|+|\mathcal{Q}_{1}(w)|+|\mathcal{Q}_{2}(w)|
.

Estimate for
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
:

Actually, the estimate :

(12.1)

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{Q}_{0}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\leq C(s,R)p^{\beta}

has somehow already been proven in the proof of Lemma
11.1
. Indeed, the proof is very similar, and we only sketch the beginning of it.

Sketch of the proof of (
12.1
).

We decompose
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
dyadically and we use Lemma
9.4
(with
\Omega(\vec{k})=0
), and we obtain :

\left|\mathcal{Q}_{0}(w)\right|\lesssim\sum_{N_{1},...,N_{6}}\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(N_{1},...,N_{6})

where,

\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(N_{1},...,N_{6}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
\Omega(\vec{k})=0\end{subarray}}N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}|w_{k_{j}}|

To estimate
\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(N_{1},...,N_{6})
, we use Lemma
9.6
(with
f^{j}_{k_{j}}=\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}|w_{k_{j}}|
), which yields:

\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(N_{1},...,N_{6})\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}N_{(3)}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{6}\left\lVert P_{N_{j}}w\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

with
P_{N}
the projector onto frequencies
|k|\sim N
. This estimate is the analogue of (
11.3
). And from this point, the proof of (
12.1
) goes exactly the same as the proof of Lemma
11.1
.
∎

To sum up, in order to prove Proposition
8.2
, it remains to establish the estimates in the following lemma :

Lemma 12.1
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}
. Then, there exists
\beta\in(0,1)
such that for every
R>0
, there exists a constant
C(s,R)>0
, such that for any
p\in[2,+\infty)
,

(12.2)

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{Q}_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\leq C(s,R)p^{\beta}

for
j=1,2
.

Since the analysis for
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is the same, we only prove the estimate for
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.

12.0.1.
Notations and remarks on set of indices:

Before decomposing
\mathcal{Q}
, let us first introduce some notations :

Notations 12.2
.

Given a set of frequency
k_{1},k_{2},...,k_{6},p_{1},p_{2},...,p_{5}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote :

•

k_{(1)},...,k_{(6)}
a rearrangement of the
k_{j}
such that

|k_{(1)}|\geq|k_{(2)}|\geq...\geq|k_{(6)}|

•

p_{(1)},...,p_{(6)}
a rearrangement of the
p_{j}
such that

|p_{(1)}|\geq|p_{(2)}|\geq...\geq|p_{(5)}|

•

n_{(1)},n_{(2)},...,n_{(10)}
a rearrangement of
k_{2},...,k_{6},p_{1},...,p_{5}
such that

|n_{(1)}|\geq|n_{(2)}|\geq...\geq|n_{(10)}|

Also, in the sequel we will use :

•

the letter
M_{j}
for the localization of the frequency
k_{j}
,

•

the letter
P_{j}
for the localization of the frequency
p_{j}
,

Finally,
N_{(1)}\geq N_{(2)}\geq...\geq N_{(10)}
will be a non-increasing rearrangement of
P_{1},...,P_{5},M_{2},...,M_{6}
.

Remark 12.3
.

– when
k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0
, we have
|k_{(1)}|\sim|k_{(2)}|
.

– when
k_{1}=p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}
, we have
|p_{(1)}|\sim|p_{(2)}|
or
|p_{(1)}|\sim|k_{1}|
.

– when
k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0
and
k_{1}=p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}
, we have
|n_{(1)}|\sim|n_{(2)}|
.

12.1.
Decomposition

For convenience, we will denote
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
simply as
\mathcal{Q}
.

Next, in the same spirit as the decomposition in Section
11
, we decompose the set of indices over which we sum in
\mathcal{Q}
. We invoke the following set of indices :

(12.3)

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{D}:=\{(k_{1},...,k_{6},p_{1},...,p_{5})&\in\mathbb{Z}^{11}:\hskip 2.84544pt\sum_{j=1}^{6}(-1)^{j-1}k_{j}=0,\hskip 5.69046ptk_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{5}(-1)^{j-1}p_{j},\hskip 5.69046pt\sum_{j=1}^{6}(-1)^{j-1}k_{j}^{2}\neq 0,\\
&(n_{(1)},n_{(2)})\in\{p_{1},...,p_{5}\}^{2}\hskip 5.69046pt\textbf{or}\hskip 5.69046pt|n_{(3)}|<|n_{(1)}|^{1-\delta_{0}}\hskip 2.84544pt\textbf{or}\hskip 5.69046pt|n_{(4)}|\geq|n_{(3)}|^{\delta_{0}}\}\end{split}

and,

(12.4)

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{W}:=\{(k_{1},...,k_{6},p_{1},...&,p_{5})\in\mathbb{Z}^{11}:\hskip 2.84544pt\sum_{j=1}^{6}(-1)^{j-1}k_{j}=0,\hskip 5.69046ptk_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{5}(-1)^{j-1}p_{j},\hskip 5.69046pt\sum_{j=1}^{6}(-1)^{j-1}k_{j}^{2}\neq 0,\\
&(n_{(1)},n_{(2)})\notin\{p_{1},...,p_{5}\}^{2}\hskip 5.69046pt\textbf{and}\hskip 5.69046pt|n_{(3)}|\geq|n_{(1)}|^{1-\delta_{0}}\hskip 2.84544pt\textbf{and}\hskip 5.69046pt|n_{(4)}|<|n_{(3)}|^{\delta_{0}}\}\end{split}

for a fixed
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
15
15

15

Once again,
\delta_{0}
can be any number in
(0,1)
. Note that what differs from the decomposition in Section
11
is the additional constraint
(n_{(1)},n_{(2)})\notin\{p_{1},...,p_{5}\}^{2}
in
\mathcal{I}_{W}
. This is because if
(n_{(1)},n_{(2)})\in\{p_{1},...,p_{5}\}^{2}
, we will not need to use Wiener-chaos.

In the same vein, we split
\mathcal{Q}
as :

\mathcal{Q}(w)=\mathcal{Q}^{(D)}(w)+\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}(w)

where,

(12.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(D)}(w)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\mathcal{I}_{D}}\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{p_{1}}\overline{w_{p_{2}}}...w_{p_{5}}\overline{w_{k_{2}}}...\overline{w_{k_{6}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}(w):=\sum_{\mathcal{I}_{W}}\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{p_{1}}\overline{w_{p_{2}}}...w_{p_{5}}\overline{w_{k_{2}}}...\overline{w_{k_{6}}}

12.1.1.
Absence of pairing

Recall that we have defined what a ”pairing” is in Definition
11.3
.

In
\mathcal{I}_{W}
(see (
12.4
) above), there is (for
n_{(1)}
large enough) no pairing within the three highest frequencies, relatively to the constraint:

p_{1}-p_{2}...+p_{5}-k_{2}+k_{3}...-k_{6}=0

Indeed, suppose there is a pairing between two of the three highest frequencies. Then, the constraint would take the form:

the remaining high frequency = sum of seven low frequencies

which is impossible because in
\mathcal{I}_{W}
we have:

|
high frequencies
|

\gg

|
low frequencies
|

12.2.
Proof of the estimate

We are now prepared to prove Lemma
12.1
(for
j=1
). The forthcoming proof is organized as follows. Firstly, we establish an estimate that will be conclusive only for the contribution
\mathcal{Q}^{(D)}
. For the contribution
\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}
, we will also exploit the independence between high frequency Gaussians and low frequency Gaussians using Wiener chaos estimate.

Proof of Lemma
12.1
.

Step 1, Deterministic estimate :

Let
w\in H^{s-\frac{1}{2}-}(\mathbb{T})
. For convenience, for any dyadic number
N
we denote
w_{k}^{N}:=\mathds{1}_{|k|\sim N}|w_{k}|
. We start by taking the absolute value and summing over dyadic blocks

\left|\mathcal{Q}(w)\right|\lesssim\sum_{M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}}\mathfrak{Q}(M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5})

where
M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}
are dyadic-valued and

\mathfrak{Q}(M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0\\
p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}\\
\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\big||w_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}...w_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}w_{k_{2}}^{M_{2}}...w_{k_{6}}^{M_{6}}|\cdot\mathds{1}_{|k_{1}|\sim M_{1}}

We decompose
\mathfrak{Q}
as
\mathfrak{Q}=\mathfrak{Q}^{(D)}+\mathfrak{Q}^{(W)}
, according to the decomposition of
\mathcal{Q}
.

In regard to (
10.4
) and (
10.6
) from the proof of Proposition
4.1
, we have:

\mathfrak{Q}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\lesssim M_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}M_{(3)}^{2}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{5}\left\lVert w^{P_{j}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\prod_{j=2}^{6}\left\lVert w^{M_{j}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

Using Notations
12.2
, it means that

(12.6)

\mathfrak{Q}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\lesssim M_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}M_{(3)}^{2}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{10}\left\lVert w^{N_{j}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

Moreover, for
1<\sigma^{\prime}<\sigma<s-\frac{1}{2}
, we have :

(12.7)

\prod_{j=1}^{10}\left\lVert w^{N_{j}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\lesssim(N_{(1)}^{-\sigma}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}})(N_{(2)}^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma^{\prime}}})(N_{(3)}N_{(4)}...N_{(10)})^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{8}

Then, since
N_{(1)}\sim N_{(2)}
, and writing
\sigma^{\prime}
as:

\sigma^{\prime}=\alpha 1+(1-\alpha)\sigma

for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we deduce from (
12.7
), by interpolating
H^{\sigma^{\prime}}
between
H^{1}
and
H^{\sigma}
, that:

\begin{split}\prod_{j=1}^{10}\left\lVert w^{N_{j}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}&\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-2\sigma+\alpha(\sigma-1)}(N_{(3)}N_{(4)}...N_{(10)})^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{8+\alpha}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}}\\
&\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-2\sigma+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}(N_{(3)}N_{(4)}...N_{(10)})^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{8+\alpha}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}},\hskip 8.5359pt(\textnormal{because $\alpha(\sigma-1)<\alpha(s-3/2)$ })\end{split}

Plugging this into (
12.6
), we obtain

\mathfrak{Q}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-2\sigma+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}M_{(1)}^{2s-2+\varepsilon}M_{(3)}^{2}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}(N_{(3)}N_{(4)}...N_{(10)})^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{8+\alpha}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}}

Using the facts
M_{(1)}\lesssim N_{(1)}
and
N_{(1)}\sim N_{(2)}
, and also Remark
7.2
, we deduce that

(12.8)

\begin{split}\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathfrak{Q}&(M_{1},...,P_{5})\\
&\lesssim_{\varepsilon,R}N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}M_{(3)}^{2}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{2}_{(1)}(\overbrace{N_{(1)}N_{(2)}N_{(3)}N_{(4)}...N_{(10)}}^{=M_{2}...M_{6}P_{1}...P_{5}})^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}}\\
&\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}P_{(1)}^{-1}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}}\\
&\lesssim\begin{cases}N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma)-\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}},\hskip 54.06006pt\textnormal{if $(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}$}\\
N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}N_{(3)}N_{(4)}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2-\alpha}_{H^{\sigma}},\hskip 11.38092pt\textnormal{if $(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\notin\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}$}\end{cases}\end{split}

Here, we used estimates on
M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}P_{(1)}^{-1}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in terms of the
N_{j}
, which are gathered in the following lemma.

Lemma 12.4
.

–

If
(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}
, then

M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}P_{(1)}^{-1}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}}

–

If
(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\notin\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}
, then

M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}P_{(1)}^{-1}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-2}N_{(3)}N_{(4)}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

For clarity, we postpone the proof of Lemma
12.4
for the end of this section.

To conclude the first step of the proof, using (
9.3
) in (
12.8
) yields:

(12.9)

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathfrak{Q}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\lesssim\begin{cases}N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma)-\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}p^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}},\hskip 54.06006pt\textnormal{if $(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}$}\\
N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}N_{(3)}N_{(4)}^{-\frac{1}{2}}p^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}},\hskip 11.38092pt\textnormal{if $(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\notin\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}$}\end{cases}

where the constant depends on
s,R,\sigma
and
\varepsilon
. This is the deterministic estimate we will work with later in the proof. We stress the fact that (
12.9
) is true for
\mathfrak{Q}
and also for
\mathfrak{Q}^{(D)}
and
\mathfrak{Q}^{(W)}
(with the exact same proof).

Step 2, Estimates for
\mathcal{Q}^{(D)}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}
:

•Let us begin with the estimate for
\mathcal{Q}^{(D)}
. Recall that in
\mathcal{Q}^{(D)}
we sum over the set
\mathcal{I}_{D}
defined in (
12.3
). And, the constraints in
\mathcal{I}_{D}
imply that the dyadic integers (from the dyadic decomposition) must satisfy one of the following conditions :

\displaystyle(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})

\displaystyle\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}

or

\displaystyle N_{(3)}

\displaystyle\lesssim N_{(1)}^{1-\delta_{0}}

or

\displaystyle N_{(4)}

\displaystyle\gtrsim N_{(3)}^{\delta_{0}}

In the first situation, when
(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}
, (
12.9
)
(with
\mathfrak{Q}^{(D)}
instead of
\mathfrak{Q}
) yields:

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathfrak{Q}^{(D)}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma)-\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}p^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}

And, the exponent of
N_{(1)}
satisfy :

\begin{cases}2(s-\sigma)-\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})>-\frac{1}{2}\\
2(s-\sigma)-\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\longrightarrow-\frac{1}{2}\hskip 5.69046pt\text{as}\begin{cases}\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\\
\alpha\rightarrow 0\\
\sigma\rightarrow s-\frac{1}{2}\end{cases}\end{cases}

Thus, for
\varepsilon
and
\alpha
close enough to
0
and
\sigma
close enough to
s-\frac{1}{2}
, we have

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathfrak{Q}^{(D)}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-}p^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}

Now, in the second situation when
(N_{(1},N_{(2)})\notin\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}

and :

N_{(3)}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{1-\delta_{0}}

or

N_{(4)}\gtrsim N_{(3)}^{\delta_{0}}
, (
12.9
) yields :

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathfrak{Q}^{(D)}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})}N_{(3)}N_{(4)}^{-\frac{1}{2}}p^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}

And since the exponent of
N_{(1)}
satisfy :

\begin{cases}2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})>-1\\
2(s-\sigma-1)+\varepsilon+\alpha(s-\frac{3}{2})\longrightarrow-1\hskip 5.69046pt\text{as}\begin{cases}\varepsilon\rightarrow 0\\
\alpha\rightarrow 0\\
\sigma\rightarrow s-\frac{1}{2}\end{cases}\end{cases}

we deduce that for a fixed
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
and for
\varepsilon
and
\alpha
close enough to
0
and
\sigma
close enough to
s-\frac{1}{2}
, we have again

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathfrak{Q}^{(D)}(M_{1},...,P_{5})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-}p^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}

Finally, summing over the dyadic integers
M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}
, this estimate for both situations leads to

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{Q}^{(D)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\lesssim p^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}}

which is the desired estimate.

•Next, in order to finish the proof of Lemma
12.1
, we need the same estimate for the contribution
\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}
. For this term, while the estimate (
12.9
) is not conclusive, we are in a situation where we can make use of Wiener chaos. Recall that in
\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}
, we sum over the set
\mathcal{I}_{W}
defined in (
12.4
). Even though the following method is very similar to the one used for estimating
\mathcal{R}^{(W)}
in Section
11
(see the proof of Lemma
11.1
), we re-perform the analysis in detail.

As usual, we start by decomposing
\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}
as
16
16

16

For the analysis below, we need to keep the complex conjugation bars, so here we don’t use
\mathfrak{Q}^{(W)}

\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}=\sum_{M_{1},...,M_{6},P1,...,P_{5}}\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}_{M_{1},...,M_{6},P1,...,P_{5}}
, where :

\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}_{M_{1},...,M_{6},P1,...,P_{5}}:=\sum_{\mathcal{I}_{W}}\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{p_{1}}\overline{w_{p_{2}}}...w_{p_{5}}\overline{w_{k_{2}}}...\overline{w_{k_{6}}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{6}\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim M_{j}}\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{5}\mathds{1}_{p_{j}\sim P_{j}}\right)

Henceforth, we denote
w_{k_{j}}^{M_{j}}:=\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim N_{j}}w_{k_{j}}
and
w_{p_{j}}^{P_{j}}:=\mathds{1}_{|p_{j}|\sim P_{j}}w_{p_{j}}
for better readability.

Without loss of generality, we only prove the estimate for the contribution in
\mathcal{Q}^{(W)}
where the three highest frequencies are
k_{2},k_{3}
and
k_{4}
, because the other cases are identical.

Again we denote
\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by Gaussians
\left(g_{k}\right)_{|k|\leq N_{(3)}/100}
. Since we only need to consider the contribution when
N_{(1)}
is large, we can assume that
N_{(1)}
is sufficiently large to ensure
N_{(4)}\leq N_{(3)}/100
. This follows from the constraints in
\mathcal{I}_{W}
which imply
N_{(3)}\gtrsim N_{(1)}^{1-\delta_{0}}
and
N_{(4)}\lesssim N_{(3)}^{\delta_{0}}
. Thus, for
N_{(1)}
large enough, we have
N_{(3)}^{\delta_{0}}\ll N_{(3)}
, ensuring that
N_{(4)}\leq N_{(3)}/100
.

As a consequence, we have that the random variables :

\begin{split}&w_{k_{2}}^{M_{2}},w_{k_{3}}^{M_{3}},w_{k_{4}}^{M_{4}}\hskip 5.69046pt\textnormal{are independent of $\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}}$},\\
&w_{k_{5}}^{M_{5}},w_{k_{6}}^{M_{6}},w_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}},w_{p_{2}}^{P_{2}},w_{p_{3}}^{P_{3}},w_{p_{4}}^{P_{4}},w_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}\hskip 5.69046pt\textnormal{are $\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}}$ mesurable}.\end{split}

Now, identically to (
11.7
), we have

(12.10)

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{Q}_{M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\leq\Big\|\big\|\mathcal{Q}_{M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}}^{(W)}\big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s}|\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}})}\cdot\mathds{1}_{B_{R}^{H^{1}}}(P_{N_{(3)/100}}u)\Big\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(d\mu_{s})}

And, the conditional Wiener-chaos estimate from Lemma
9.9
(with
m=3
), followed by Lemma
9.10
combined with the absence of paring (see Paragraph
12.1.1
), allows us to obtain :

(12.11)

\begin{split}&\left\lVert\mathcal{Q}_{M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s}|\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}})}\lesssim p^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert\mathcal{Q}_{M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(d\mu_{s}|\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}})}\\
&\lesssim p^{\frac{3}{2}}(N_{(1)}N_{(2)}N_{(3)})^{-s}\bigg(\sum_{k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}}\Big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{5},k_{6}\\
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5}\end{subarray}}C(\vec{k},\vec{p})\cdot\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}\overline{w_{p_{2}}^{P_{2}}}w_{p_{3}}^{P_{3}}\overline{w_{p_{4}}^{P_{4}}}w_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}w_{k_{5}}^{M_{5}}\overline{w_{k_{6}}^{M_{6}}}\Big|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\end{split}

where we gathered all the constraints into the term :

C(\vec{k},\vec{p}):=\mathds{1}_{k_{1}-k_{2}+...-k_{6}=0}\cdot\mathds{1}_{p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}}\cdot\mathds{1}_{\Omega(\vec{k})\neq 0}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{6}\mathds{1}_{|k_{j}|\sim M_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{5}\mathds{1}_{|p_{j}|\sim P_{j}}

Next, by Cauchy-Schwarz,

\begin{split}&\sum_{k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}}\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{5},k_{6}\\
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5}\end{subarray}}C(\vec{k},\vec{p})\cdot\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}...w_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}w_{k_{5}}^{M_{5}}\overline{w_{k_{6}}^{M_{6}}}\bigg|^{2}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{5},k_{6}\\
p_{1},p_{2},p_{2},p_{4},p_{5}\end{subarray}}C(\vec{k},\vec{p})\cdot\Big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\Big|^{2}|w_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}...w_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}w_{k_{5}}^{M_{5}}|^{2}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{5},k_{6}\\
p_{1},p_{2},p_{2},p_{4},p_{5}\end{subarray}}C(\vec{k},\vec{p})|w_{k_{6}}^{M_{6}}|^{2}\bigg)\end{split}

From Lemma
9.4
we get
\left|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\right|\lesssim|k_{(1)}|^{2s-2}(1+\frac{|k_{(3)}|^{2}}{|\Omega(\vec{k})|})\lesssim|k_{(1)}|^{2s-2}|k_{(3)}|^{2}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}
. Then, on the one hand:

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{5},k_{6}\\
p_{1},p_{2},p_{2},p_{4},p_{5}\end{subarray}}C(\vec{k},\vec{p})\cdot&\Big|\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}\Big|^{2}|w_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}...w_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}w_{k_{5}}^{M_{5}}|^{2}\lesssim(N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2})^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{5},k_{6}\\
p_{1},p_{2},p_{2},p_{4},p_{5}\end{subarray}}C(\vec{k},\vec{p})\cdot|w_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}...w_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}w_{k_{5}}^{M_{5}}|^{2}\\
&\lesssim(N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2})^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{5}\\
p_{1},p_{2},p_{2},p_{4},p_{5}\end{subarray}}|w_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}...w_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}w_{k_{5}}^{M_{5}}|^{2}\cdot\underbrace{\sum_{k_{1},k_{6}}\mathds{1}_{k_{6}=k_{1}-k_{2}...+k_{5}}\cdot\mathds{1}_{p_{1}-p_{2}+...+p_{5}=k_{1}}}_{\leq 1}\\
&\lesssim(N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}\left\lVert w^{P_{1}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}...\left\lVert w^{P_{5}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\lVert w^{M_{5}}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}})^{2}\\
&\lesssim\left(N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}(P_{1}...P_{5}M_{5})^{-1}\left\lVert w^{P_{1}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}...\left\lVert w^{P_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{M_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\right)^{2}\end{split}

and on the other hand,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},k_{5},k_{6}\\
p_{1},p_{2},p_{2},p_{4},p_{5}\end{subarray}}C(\vec{k},\vec{p})|w_{k_{6}}^{N_{6}}|^{2}=\sum_{k_{6}}|w_{k_{6}}^{N_{6}}|^{2}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4},k_{5}\\
p_{1},p_{2},p_{2},p_{4},p_{5}\end{subarray}}C(\vec{k},\vec{p})\lesssim N_{(2)}N_{(3)}P_{1}...P_{5}M_{5}\cdot(M_{6}^{-1}\left\lVert w^{M_{6}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}})^{2}

where we used the counting bound from Lemma
9.3
. We deduce from the two inequalities above that

\begin{split}\biggl(\sum_{k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}}\big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{5},k_{6}\\
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5}\end{subarray}}&C(\vec{k},\vec{p})\cdot\frac{\psi_{2s}(\vec{k})}{\Omega(\vec{k})}w_{p_{1}}^{P_{1}}\overline{w_{p_{2}}^{P_{2}}}w_{p_{3}}^{P_{3}}\overline{w_{p_{4}}^{P_{4}}}w_{p_{5}}^{P_{5}}w_{k_{5}}^{M_{5}}\overline{w_{k_{6}}^{M_{6}}}\big|^{2}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2s-2}N_{(3)}^{2}N_{(1)}^{\frac{1}{2}}N_{(3)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert w^{P_{1}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}...\left\lVert w^{P_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{M_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{M_{6}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\\
&\lesssim N_{(1)}^{2s-\frac{3}{2}}N_{(3)}^{\frac{5}{2}}\left\lVert w^{P_{1}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}...\left\lVert w^{P_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{M_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{M_{6}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\end{split}

Coming back to (
12.11
), we deduce that

\left\lVert\mathcal{Q}_{M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s}|\mathcal{B}_{\ll N_{(3)}})}\lesssim p^{\frac{3}{2}}N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}}N_{(3)}^{\frac{5}{2}-s}\left\lVert w^{P_{1}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}...\left\lVert w^{P_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{M_{5}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert w^{M_{6}}\right\rVert_{H^{1}}

And plugging this into (
12.10
), we obtain :

\left\lVert\mathds{1}_{\{\mathcal{C}(u)\leq R\}}\mathcal{Q}_{M_{1},...,M_{6},P_{1},...,P_{5}}^{(W)}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{p}(d\mu_{s})}\lesssim_{R}p^{\frac{3}{2}}N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}}N_{(3)}^{\frac{5}{2}-s}

Interpolating the above inequality with (
12.9
) (more precisely (
12.9
) with
\mathfrak{Q}^{(W)}
instead of
\mathfrak{Q}
), we can conclude in the same way as what we did with
\mathcal{R}^{(W)}
in the proof of Lemma
11.1
(in particular using again Lemma
11.5
).
∎

In order to complete the proof of Lemma
12.1
, we provide in the next paragraph a proof of the technical Lemma
12.4
.

Proof of Lemma
12.4

Proof of Lemma
12.4
.

Recall that we use Notations
12.2
.

•Assume first that
(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}
. Then,
P_{(1)}^{-1}P_{(2)}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}}
. Moreover, we have

M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}\leq M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{(2)}M_{(3)}...M_{(6)})^{-1}\leq(M_{(4)}M_{(5)}M_{(6)})^{-1}\lesssim 1

Combining these two inequalities, we obtain

M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}P_{(1)}^{-1}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}}(P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-\frac{3}{2}}

which is the desired estimate.

•Now, assume that
(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\notin\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}^{2}
. It means that :

\begin{split}(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\in\{M_{2},...,M_{6}\}^{2}\hskip 5.69046pt&\textbf{or}\left(\hskip 5.69046ptN_{(1)}\in\{M_{2},...,M_{6}\}\hskip 5.69046pt\textbf{and}\hskip 5.69046ptN_{(2)}\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}\hskip 5.69046pt\right)\hskip 5.69046pt\\
&\textbf{or}\left(\hskip 5.69046ptN_{(2)}\in\{M_{2},...,M_{6}\}\hskip 5.69046pt\textbf{and}\hskip 5.69046ptN_{(1)}\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}\hskip 5.69046pt\right)\end{split}

– Fristly, we suppose that
(N_{(1)},N_{(2)})\in\{M_{2},...,M_{6}\}^{2}
. Then, we note that
N_{(3)}\in\{M_{2},...,M_{6}\}

or

P_{(1)}=N_{(3)}
. And also,

N_{(4)}\in\{M_{2},...,M_{6}\}\hskip 11.38092pt\textbf{or}\hskip 11.38092ptN_{(4)}\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}

Considering these facts (and still the fact that
N_{(1)}\sim N_{(2)}
), we deduce that

M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}P_{(1)}^{-1}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim M_{(3)}^{2}N_{(1)}^{-2}N_{(3)}^{-1}N_{(4)}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

Using then the fact that
M_{(3)}\lesssim N_{(3)}
, we have

M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}P_{(1)}^{-1}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-2}N_{(3)}N_{(4)}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

which is the desired inequality.

–Secondly, we suppose that
N_{(1)}\in\{M_{2},...,M_{6}\}\hskip 5.69046pt\textbf{and}\hskip 5.69046ptN_{(2)}\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}
. We consider two cases.

a)
The first one is when
M_{1}\in\{M_{(1)},M_{(2)},M_{(3)}\}
. In that case,
M_{(4)},M_{(5)},M_{(6)},P_{(2)},...,P_{(5)}
are seven indices out of the ten indices
N_{j}
(that would not have been the case if
M_{(1)}\in\{M_{(4)},M_{(5)},M_{(6)}\}
, because according to Notations
12.2
,
M_{1}
is not one of the
N_{j}
). As a consequence,

\max\{M_{(4)},M_{(5)},M_{(6)},P_{(2)},...,P_{(5)}\}\geq N_{(4)}\hskip 8.5359pt\textnormal{so,}\hskip 8.5359pt(M_{(4)}M_{(5)}M_{(6)})^{-1}(P_{(2)}...P_{(5)})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N_{(4)}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

On the other hand, using the fact that
M_{(1)}\sim M_{(2)}
, we have

M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}M_{3}...M_{6})^{-1}\lesssim M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{(2)}M_{(3)}...M_{(6)})^{-1}\lesssim M_{(1)}^{-1}M_{(3)}(M_{(4)}M_{(5)}M_{(6)})^{-1}

Combining these two inequalities and recalling that
M_{(1)},P_{(1)}\sim N_{(1)}
, we obtain

M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}P_{(1)}^{-1}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-2}M_{(3)}N_{(4)}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-2}N_{(3)}N_{(4)}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

b)
The second case we consider is when
M_{1}\in\{M_{(4)},M_{(5)},M_{(6)}\}
. In that case,
\{M_{(1)},M_{(2)},M_{(3)}\}\subset\{M_{2},M_{3},...,M_{6}\}
. Thus,

(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}\lesssim(M_{(1)}M_{(2)}M_{(3)}M_{(5)}M_{(6)})^{-1}

so,

M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-2}M_{(3)}

and,

M_{(3)}^{2}(M_{2}...M_{6})^{-1}P_{(1)}^{-1}\left(P_{(2)}P_{(3)}P_{(4)}P_{(5)}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-3}M_{(3)}\lesssim N_{(1)}^{-3}N_{(3)}

which is an even better estimate than the one we desired.

–Finally, the case
N_{(2)}\in\{M_{2},...,M_{6}\}\hskip 5.69046pt\textbf{and}\hskip 5.69046ptN_{(1)}\in\{P_{1},...,P_{5}\}
is identical to the previous one.

Hence, the proof of Lemma
12.4
is finished.

∎

Appendix A
Construction and properties of the flow and the truncated flow

In this appendix, we study the local and global wellposedness in
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
,
\sigma\geq 1
, for both equation (
1.1
) and truncated equation (
2.1
). We also study approximation properties of the truncated flow along with its structure.

A.1.
Local and global wellposedness

We begin this paragraph by recalling some facts about the space :

W(\mathbb{T}):=\{u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}):\hskip 2.84544pt\underset{k\in\mathbb{Z}}{\sum}\hskip 2.84544pt|\widehat{u}(k)|<+\infty\}

of absolutely convergent Fourier series, called the
Wiener algebra
, and equipped with the norm

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{W(\mathbb{T})}:=\underset{k\in\mathbb{Z}}{\sum}\hskip 2.84544pt|\widehat{u}(k)|

(1)

W(\mathbb{T})
is a Banach algebra with the estimate

\left\lVert uv\right\rVert_{W(\mathbb{T})}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{W(\mathbb{T})}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{W(\mathbb{T})}

(2)

For every
\sigma\geq 0
,
W(\mathbb{T})\cap H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
is an algebra. Furthermore, there exists
C_{\sigma}>0
such that

\left\lVert uv\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq C_{\sigma}(\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{W(\mathbb{T})}+\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{W(\mathbb{T})})

In particular, if
\sigma>\frac{1}{2}
,
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
is a Banach algebra and there exists
C^{\prime}_{\sigma}>0
such that

\left\lVert uv\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq C^{\prime}_{\sigma}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}

We are now ready to prove the following local existence theorem :

Theorem A.1
.

Let
\sigma\geq 1
. Let
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Both equation (
1.1
) and truncated equation (
2.1
) are locally well-posed in
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
, in the sense that for any
R_{0}>0
, there exists
T_{0}>0
such that for every
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq R_{0}
there exists a unique
u\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0}-T_{0},t_{0}+T_{0}],H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}))
that satisfies the Duhamel formula :

(A.1)

u(t)=e^{i(t-t_{0})\partial_{x}^{2}}u_{0}-i\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}(|u(\tau)|^{4}u(\tau))d\tau

for all
|t-t_{0}|\leq T_{0}
. And, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a unique
v_{N}\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0}-T_{0},t_{0}+T_{0}],H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}))
that satisfies the Duhamel formula :

(A.2)

v_{N}(t)=e^{i(t-t_{0})\partial_{x}^{2}}u_{0}-i\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}(|\Pi_{N}v_{N}(\tau)|^{4}\Pi_{N}v_{N}(\tau))d\tau

for all
|t-t_{0}|\leq T_{0}
.

Remark A.2
.

It is important to notice that the existence time
T_{0}>0
is the same for both NLS and truncated equation and does not depend on the integer
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

To show Theorem
A.1
, we apply a fixed point argument. Fix
R_{0}>0
and
||u_{0}||_{H^{\sigma}}\leq R_{0}
. For
T>0
, we denote

X_{T}:=\mathcal{C}([t_{0}-T,t_{0},+T],H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}))

which is a Banach space when endowed with the sup norm

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{X_{T}}:=\underset{\tau\in[t_{0}-T,t_{0},+T]}{\textnormal{sup}}\left\lVert u(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}

For every
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, let us consider the well-defined map

\begin{split}\Gamma_{N}:\hskip 2.84544pt&X_{T}\longrightarrow X_{T}\\
&u\longmapsto e^{i(t-t_{0})\partial_{x}^{2}}u_{0}-i\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}(|\Pi_{N}u(\tau)|^{4}\Pi_{N}u(\tau))d\tau\end{split}

where by convention
\Pi_{\infty}=id
. We recall the crucial point that
||\Pi_{N}u||_{H^{\sigma}}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}
. Thanks to that point, every estimate below are uniform in
N
so that every constant below does not depend on N.

Using now the facts that
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
is an algebra (since
\sigma\geq 1>\frac{1}{2}
) and
e^{i(t-t_{0})\partial_{x}^{2}}
is a linear isometry on
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
, we have for some constant
C_{0}>0
,

(A.3)

\left\lVert\Gamma_{N}u\right\rVert_{X_{T}}\leq\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+C_{0}T\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{X_{T}}^{5}

In addition, using the multilinearity of
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4},u_{5})\longmapsto u_{1}\overline{u_{2}}u_{3}\overline{u_{4}}u_{5}
we have for some constant
C_{1}>0
,

(A.4)

\begin{split}\left\lVert\Gamma_{N}u-\Gamma_{N}v\right\rVert_{X_{T}}&=\left\lVert\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}(|\Pi_{N}u(\tau)|^{4}\Pi_{N}u(\tau)-|\Pi_{N}v(\tau)|^{4}\Pi_{N}v(\tau))d\tau\right\rVert_{X_{T}}\\
&\leq C_{1}T(\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{4}_{X_{T}}+\left\lVert v\right\rVert^{4}_{X_{T}})\left\lVert u-v\right\rVert_{X_{T}}\end{split}

Let
R>2R_{0}
(for example
R=1+2R_{0}
) and
T_{0}:=\frac{1}{3\text{max}(C_{0},C_{1})R^{4}}
. Fix
0<T\leq T_{0}
and denote by
\overline{B}_{R}(T)
the closed centered ball in
X_{T}
of radius
R
. We get from (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) and our choice of the parameters
R
and
T_{0}
that for any
u,v\in\overline{B}_{R}(T)
,

(A.5)

||\Gamma_{N}u||_{\overline{B}_{R}(T)}\leq\frac{R}{2}+\frac{R}{2}\leq R

and,

(A.6)

||\Gamma_{N}u-\Gamma_{N}v||_{\overline{B}_{R}(T)}\leq 2C_{1}TR^{4}\left\lVert u-v\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R}(T)}\leq\frac{2}{3}\left\lVert u-v\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R}(T)}

These two estimates imply that the map
\Gamma_{N}
is a contraction from (the complete space)
\overline{B}_{R}(T)
to itself. Hence, applying the Banach’s fixed point theorem to the map

\Gamma_{N}:\overline{B}_{R}(T)\longrightarrow\overline{B}_{R}(T)

leads to the existence part of Theorem
A.1
. However, through classical considerations, we can prove that the uniqueness holds in the entire space
\mathcal{C}([t_{0}-T,t_{0}+T],H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}))
. So the proof is completed.
∎

Putting together the local solutions from Theorem
A.1
, we obtain the two following corollaries.

Corollary A.3
.

Let
\sigma\geq 1
. For every
u_{0}\in H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
, there exists a unique maximal solution
u\in\mathcal{C}(I_{max}(u_{0}),H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}))
of (
1.1
) where
I_{max}(u_{0})
is an open interval containing 0. Moreover, if
I_{max}(u_{0})
is strictly included in
\mathbb{R}
, then

\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\longrightarrow+\infty

as
t\longrightarrow\partial I_{max}(u_{0})
.

Corollary A.4
.

Let
\sigma\geq 1
. For every
u_{0}\in H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
and every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a unique maximal solution
u_{N}\in\mathcal{C}(I_{max,N}(u_{0}),H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}))
of (
2.1
) where
I_{max,N}(u_{0})
is an open interval containing 0. Moreover, if
I_{max,N}(u_{0})
is strictly included in
\mathbb{R}
, then

||u_{N}(t)||_{H^{\sigma}}\longrightarrow+\infty

as
t\longrightarrow\partial I_{max,N}(u_{0})
.

Thus, to obtain that the solutions of equations (
1.1
) and (
2.1
) are global, it suffices to show that the
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
-norm of the solutions do not blow up in finite time. Fortunately, we have :

Proposition A.5
.

Let
u_{0}\in H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
. There exists a constant
C_{0}>0
only depending on
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})}
and
\sigma\geq 1
, such that

(A.7)

\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}e^{C_{0}|t|},\hskip 2.84544pt\hskip 2.84544pt\textnormal{for all}\hskip 2.84544ptt\in I_{max}(u_{0})

and,

(A.8)

||u_{N}(t)||_{H^{\sigma}}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}e^{C_{0}|t|},\hskip 2.84544pt\hskip 2.84544pt\textnormal{for all}\hskip 2.84544ptt\in I_{max,N}(u_{0})

where
u
is the maximal solution of (
1.1
) and
u_{N}
is the maximal solution of (
2.1
). Moreover, we can explicitly choose
C_{0}\leq C_{\sigma}(1+\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})})^{12}
where
C_{\sigma}>0
depends only on
\sigma
.

Proof.

We use the convention
\Pi_{\infty}=id
,
u_{\infty}=u
and
I_{max,\infty}(u_{0})=I_{max}(u_{0})
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and
t\in I_{max,N}(u_{0})
. Passing to the
H^{\sigma}
-norm in the Duhamel formula

u_{N}(t)=e^{i(t)\partial_{x}^{2}}u_{0}-i\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}(|\Pi_{N}u_{N}(\tau)|^{4}\Pi_{N}u_{N}(\tau))d\tau

we get that, for some constant
C>0
(only depending on
\sigma
),

(A.9)

\begin{split}||u_{N}(t)||_{H^{\sigma}}&\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+\mid\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert|\Pi_{N}u_{N}(\tau)|^{4}\Pi_{N}u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}d\tau\mid\\
&\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+C\mid\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\left\lVert u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert^{4}_{W(\mathbb{T})}d\tau\mid\end{split}

The crucial point of the proof is that
\left\lVert u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert_{W(\mathbb{T})}
is bounded by the
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
-norm of the initial data. Indeed, using the mass and Hamiltonian conservation

\begin{split}\left\lVert u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert_{W(\mathbb{T})}&\leq\left\lVert u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\\
&\leq(\left\lVert u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\lVert\partial_{x}u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim\left(\left\lVert u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\partial_{x}u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\frac{1}{6}\left\lVert u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert^{6}_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim(\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\partial_{x}u_{0}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\frac{1}{6}\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{6}_{\mathbb{L}^{6}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C^{\prime}_{0}\end{split}

where we can choose
C^{\prime}_{0}\sim(1+\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})})^{3}
, because
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
embeds continuously in
\mathbb{L}^{6}(\mathbb{T})
. Now, plugging this bound into (
A.9
), we get

||u_{N}(t)||_{H^{\sigma}}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+C_{0}\mid\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert u_{N}(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}d\tau\mid

where we can choose
C_{0}\sim(1+\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})})^{12}
. Then, applying Gronwall’s inequality, we obtain

||u_{N}(t)||_{H^{\sigma}}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}e^{C_{0}|t|}

which is the desired bound.
∎

Theorem A.6
.

For every
u_{0}\in H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
, each maximal solution of (
1.1
) and (
2.1
) is global. In other words,

I_{max}(u_{0}),I_{max,N}(u_{0})=+\infty

for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The estimates from Proposition
A.5
imply that the
H^{\sigma}
-norm of both solutions does not blow up in finite time. Therefore, Corollaries
A.3
and
A.4
lead to the result.
∎

A.2.
Regularity of the flows and approximation properties

We recall that we denote by
\Phi(t)
the flow of (
1.1
) and by
\Phi_{N}(t)
the flow of the truncated equation (
2.1
). We also use the notation
\Phi_{\infty}(t)=\Phi(t)
.

Proposition A.7
.

Let
\sigma\geq 1
. Let
R>0
and
T>0
. There exists a constant
\Lambda(R,T)>0
only depending on
T,R
and
\sigma
, such that for any
u_{0}\in B_{R}^{H^{\sigma}}
,

\sup_{|t|\leq T}\left\lVert\Phi(t)u_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+\sup_{|t|\leq T}\left\lVert\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq\Lambda(R,T),\hskip 2.84544pt\hskip 2.84544pt\forall N\in\mathbb{N}

Proof.

This is a consequence of Proposition
A.5
. For example we can take

\Lambda(R,T)=Re^{C_{\sigma}(1+R)^{12}T}

for a certain constant
C_{\sigma}>0
only depending on
\sigma
.
∎

We continue this paragraph with two significant regularity results for the flow of (
1.1
) and (
2.1
).

Proposition A.8
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
For every
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the map

\begin{split}\Phi_{N}(t):\hskip 2.84544pt&H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})\longrightarrow H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})\\
&u\longmapsto\Phi_{N}(t)u\end{split}

is continuous.

Proof.

For better readability, we only provide the proof for
\Phi(t)
.
Let
u,v\in H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq R
and
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq R
for some
R>0
. We invoke
\Lambda(R,t)>0
from the Proposition
A.7
. Firstly, using the Duhamel formula and passing to the
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
-norm we get that for all
s\leq t

\left\lVert\Phi(s)v-\Phi(s)u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq\left\lVert v-u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+C\Lambda(R,t)^{4}\mid\int_{0}^{s}\left\lVert\Phi(\tau)v-\Phi(\tau)u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}d\tau\mid

Secondly, the Gronwall’s inequality yields

\left\lVert\Phi(t)v-\Phi(t)u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq\left\lVert v-u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}e^{C\Lambda(R,t)^{4}|t|}

In particular,

\left\lVert\Phi(t)v-\Phi(t)u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\underset{v\rightarrow u}{\longrightarrow}0

∎

We can go even further with the following proposition:

Proposition A.9
.

For every
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the map

\begin{split}\Phi_{N}:\hskip 2.84544pt\mathbb{R}&\times H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})\longrightarrow H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})\\
&(t,u)\longmapsto\Phi_{N}(t)u\end{split}

is continuous.

Proof.

For better readability, we only provide the proof for
\Phi
.
We fix
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
u\in H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
and we show that

\left\lVert\Phi(t+h)(u+\delta u)-\Phi(t)u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\longrightarrow 0,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as $h\rightarrow 0$ and $\delta u\rightarrow 0$}

We denote
F(\tau,v):=|\Phi(\tau)v|^{4}\Phi(\tau)v
. Let us compute the difference
\Phi(t+h)(u+\delta u)-\Phi(t)u
using the Duhamel formula.

\begin{split}&\Phi(t+h)(u+\delta u)-\Phi(t)u=\Bigl(\Phi(t+h)(u+\delta u)-\Phi(t+h)u\Bigr)+\Bigl(\Phi(t+h)u-\Phi(t)u\Bigr)\\
&=e^{i(t+h)\partial_{x}^{2}}\delta u-i\int_{0}^{t+h}e^{i(t+h-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}(F(\tau,u+\delta u)-F(\tau,u))d\tau\\
&+e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}(e^{ih\partial_{x}^{2}}-1)u-i\int_{0}^{t+h}e^{i(t+h-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}F(\tau,u)d\tau+i\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}F(\tau,u)d\tau\\
&=e^{i(t+h)\partial_{x}^{2}}\delta u+e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}(e^{ih\partial_{x}^{2}}-1)u-i\int_{0}^{t+h}e^{i(t+h-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}(F(\tau,u+\delta u)-F(\tau,u))d\tau\\
&-i\int_{t}^{t+h}e^{i(t+h-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}F(\tau,u)d\tau-i\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}(e^{ih\partial_{x}^{2}}-1)F(\tau,u)d\tau\end{split}

Furthermore, without loss of generality we can assume that
t+h\leq t+1=:T
and
\left\lVert u+\delta u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+1=:R
. Now, we invoke
\Lambda(R,T)>0
from Proposition
A.7
. Passing to the
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
-norm in the above formula we have

\begin{split}\left\lVert\Phi(t+h)(u+\delta u)-\Phi(t)u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}&\leq\left\lVert\delta u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+\left\lVert(e^{ih\partial_{x}^{2}}-1)u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\\
&+C\Lambda(R,T)^{4}\mid\int_{0}^{T}\left\lVert\Phi(\tau)(u+\delta u)-\Phi(\tau)u\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}d\tau\mid\\
&+|h|\Lambda(R,T)^{5}+\mid\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert(e^{ih\partial_{x}^{2}}-1)F(\tau,u)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}d\tau\mid\end{split}

Using the continuity of
v\longmapsto\Phi(\tau)v
(Proposition
A.8
) and the dominated convergence theorem, we deduce that the right hand side tends to
0
as
h\rightarrow 0
and
\delta u\rightarrow 0
. So the proof of Proposition
A.9
is complete.
∎

Proposition A.10

(Approximation property)
.

Let
\sigma\geq 1
. Let
K
be a compact subset of
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
and
T>0
. Then, uniformly in
|t|\leq T
and
u_{0}\in K
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\left\lVert\Phi(t)u_{0}-\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}=0,

In other words,

\sup_{|t|\leq T}\hskip 2.84544pt\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\hskip 2.84544pt\left\lVert\Phi(t)u_{0}-\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\underset{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Proof.

First, from the compactness of
K
, we invoke
R>0
such that
K\subset B_{R}^{H^{\sigma}}
, where
B_{R}^{H^{\sigma}}
is the closed centered ball of radius
R
in
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
. We then invoke
\Lambda(R,T)>0
from Proposition
A.7
. Now, we set
R_{1}:=1+2\Lambda(R,T)
and invoke
\delta=\delta(R_{1})>0
the local existence time associated to
R_{1}
from the local theory (Theorem
A.1
). These parameters ensure that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and
u_{0}\in B_{R}^{H^{\sigma}}
, the Duhamel map,

\begin{split}\Gamma_{N}:\hskip 2.84544pt&\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)\longrightarrow\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)\\
&u\longmapsto e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}u_{0}-i\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}(|\Pi_{N}u(\tau)|^{4}\Pi_{N}u(\tau))d\tau\end{split}

is a contraction, with a universal contraction coefficient
0<\gamma<1
(for example
\gamma=\frac{2}{3}
as in the proof of Theorem
A.1
), where
\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)
is the closed centered ball in
\mathcal{C}([-\delta,\delta],H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}))
of radius
R_{1}
.

Step 1: Local-time convergence

Firstly, we prove the local property

(A.10)

\sup_{|t|\leq\delta}\hskip 2.84544pt\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\hskip 2.84544pt\left\lVert\Phi(t)u_{0}-\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\underset{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Let
u_{0}\in B_{R}^{H^{\sigma}}
. We denote
u(t)=\Phi(t)u_{0}
and
u_{N}(t)=\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}
. Since
u
and
u_{N}
are respectively the fixed point of
\Gamma_{\infty}
and
\Gamma_{N}
, we have

\begin{split}\left\lVert u-u_{N}\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}&=\left\lVert\Gamma_{\infty}u-\Gamma_{N}u_{N}\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}\\
&\leq\left\lVert\Gamma_{\infty}u-\Gamma_{N}u\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}+\left\lVert\Gamma_{N}u-\Gamma_{N}u_{N}\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}\\
&\leq\left\lVert\Gamma_{\infty}u-\Gamma_{N}u\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}+\gamma\left\lVert u-u_{N}\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}\end{split}

Therefore,

\left\lVert u-u_{N}\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\left\lVert\Gamma_{\infty}u-\Gamma_{N}u\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}

Hence, to prove (
A.10
), it suffices to show that

\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\left\lVert\Gamma_{\infty}u-\Gamma_{N}u\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}\underset{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

Next, for every
|t|\leq\delta
we have,

\begin{split}&\Gamma_{\infty}u(t)-\Gamma_{N}u(t)=-i\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}(|u(\tau)|^{4}u(\tau)-\Pi_{N}(|\Pi_{N}u(\tau)|^{4}\Pi_{N}u(\tau)))d\tau\\
&=-i\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(|u(\tau)|^{4}u(\tau))d\tau-i\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}(|u(\tau)|^{4}u(\tau)-|\Pi_{N}u(\tau)|^{4}\Pi_{N}u(\tau))d\tau\end{split}

So passing to the sup norm we get,

\begin{split}\left\lVert\Gamma_{\infty}u-\Gamma_{N}u\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}&\leq\delta\hskip 2.84544pt\sup_{|\tau|\leq\delta}\left\lVert\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(|u(\tau)|^{4}u(\tau))\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\\
&+C\delta\hskip 2.84544pt\sup_{|\tau|\leq\delta}\left\lVert u(\tau)-\Pi_{N}u(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}(\left\lVert u(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{4}+\left\lVert\Pi_{N}u(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}^{4})\\
&\leq C\delta(\sup_{|\tau|\leq\delta}\left\lVert\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(|u(\tau)|^{4}u(\tau))\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+\sup_{|\tau|\leq\delta}\left\lVert\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\Lambda(R,T)^{4})\end{split}

Taking the supremum over
u_{0}\in K
we then obtain,

(A.11)

\begin{split}\sup_{u_{0}\in K}&\left\lVert\Gamma_{\infty}u-\Gamma_{N}u\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}\\
&\leq C\delta(\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\sup_{|\tau|\leq\delta}\left\lVert\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(|u(\tau)|^{4}u(\tau))\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+\Lambda(R,T)^{4}\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\sup_{|\tau|\leq\delta}\left\lVert\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}})\end{split}

Besides, based on a classical result in functional analysis (see Lemma
3.4
),
\Pi_{N}^{\perp}
satisfies the key property of converging uniformly to
0
on compact sets as
N\rightarrow+\infty
. At the same time, the two following sets

\displaystyle K_{1}:

\displaystyle=\{\Phi(\tau)u_{0}:\hskip 2.84544ptu_{0}\in K,\hskip 2.84544pt|\tau|\leq\delta\},

\displaystyle K_{2}:

\displaystyle=\{|\Phi(\tau)u_{0}|^{4}\Phi(\tau)u_{0}:\hskip 2.84544ptu_{0}\in K,\hskip 2.84544pt|\tau|\leq\delta\}

are compacts in
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
. Indeed, it results from the facts that the map

\begin{split}&\mathbb{R}\times H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})\longrightarrow H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})\\
&(t,u_{0})\longmapsto\Phi(t)u_{0}\end{split}

and the map

\begin{split}&H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})^{5}\longrightarrow H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})\\
(u_{1},u_{2}&,u_{3},u_{4},u_{5})\longmapsto u_{1}\overline{u_{2}}u_{3}\overline{u_{4}}u_{5}\end{split}

are continuous and the fact that the image of a compact set under a continuous map is compact. Now, rewriting (
A.11
) we get

\begin{split}\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\left\lVert\Gamma_{\infty}u-\Gamma_{N}u\right\rVert_{\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}&\leq C\delta(\sup_{w_{2}\in K_{2}}\Pi_{N}^{\perp}w_{2}+\Lambda(R,T)^{4}\sup_{w_{1}\in K_{1}}\Pi_{N}^{\perp}w_{1})\\
&\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\end{split}

And this implies the desired local property (
A.10
).

Step 2: Long-time convergence

Secondly, we complete the proof of Proposition
A.10
by iterating this local argument. Let
m:=\lfloor\frac{T}{\delta}\rfloor+1
, and for any integer
|k|\leq m
, let
I_{k}:=[k\delta-\delta,k\delta+\delta]
. We show that for any
|k|\leq
m,

(A.12)

\sup_{t\in I_{k}}\hskip 2.84544pt\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\hskip 2.84544pt\left\lVert\Phi(t)u_{0}-\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\underset{N\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

If we do so, the proof of Proposition
A.10
will be completed. At this stage, we know that (
A.12
) is true when
k=0
. Now, we assume that (
A.12
) is true for some integer
|k|\leq m-1
and we show that (
A.12
) is still true for every integer
|k^{\prime}|=|k|+1
. Also, without loss of generality, we assume that
k\geq 0
and
k^{\prime}=k+1
. The crucial point here is that since we chose
R_{1}=1+2\Lambda(R,T)
, we have that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and
u_{0}\in B_{R}^{H^{\sigma}}
, the Duhamel map

\begin{split}\Gamma_{N}:\hskip 2.84544pt&\overline{B}_{R_{1}}^{(k)}(\delta)\longrightarrow\overline{B}^{k}_{R_{1}}(\delta)\\
&u\longmapsto e^{i(t-t_{k})\partial_{x}^{2}}u_{N}(t_{k})-i\int_{t_{k}}^{t}e^{i(t-\tau)\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}(|\Pi_{N}u(\tau)|^{4}\Pi_{N}u(\tau))d\tau\end{split}

is a contraction with a universal contraction coefficient
0<\gamma<1
, where
t_{k}:=k\delta
,
u_{\infty}(t_{k}):=u(t_{k})
, and
\overline{B}_{R_{1}}^{(k)}(\delta)
is the closed centered ball in
\mathcal{C}([t_{k}-\delta,t_{k}+\delta],H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}))
of radius
R_{1}
.

Now, doing the same calculations as in the first step of the proof we obtain,

\left\lVert u-u_{N}\right\rVert_{\overline{B}^{(k)}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\left\lVert\Gamma_{\infty}u-\Gamma_{N}u\right\rVert_{\overline{B}^{(k)}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}

and,

\begin{split}\sup_{u_{0}\in K}&\left\lVert\Gamma_{\infty}u-\Gamma_{N}u\right\rVert_{\overline{B}^{(k)}_{R_{1}}(\delta)}\leq\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\sup_{t\in I_{k}}\left\lVert e^{i(t-t_{k})\partial_{x}^{2}}(u(t_{k})-u_{N}(t_{k}))\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\\
&+C\delta(\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\sup_{\tau\in I_{k}}\left\lVert\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(|u(\tau)|^{4}u(\tau))\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}+\Lambda(R,T)^{4}\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\sup_{\tau\in I_{k}}\left\lVert\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u(\tau)\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}})\end{split}

On the right hand side, the first term :

\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\sup_{t\in I_{k}}\left\lVert e^{i(t-t_{k})\partial_{x}^{2}}(u(t_{k})-u_{N}(t_{k}))\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}=\sup_{u_{0}\in K}\left\lVert u(t_{k})-u_{N}(t_{k})\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}

tends to
0
as
N\longrightarrow+\infty
by our assumption. And, we handle the remaining term in the same way as in the first part of the proof. Finally (
A.12
) is true for all
|k|\leq m
, so the proof of Proposition
A.10
is completed.
∎

Corollary A.11
.

Let
\sigma\geq 1
,
T>0
and let
K
be a compact subset of
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
. Then,

(1)

for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}

\Phi_{N}(t)(K)\subset\Phi(t)(K)+B_{\varepsilon}^{H^{\sigma}}

for all
|t|\leq T
.

(2)

for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
N\geq N_{1}

\Phi(t)(K)\subset\Phi_{N}(t)(K+B_{\varepsilon}^{H^{\sigma}})

for all
|t|\leq T
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
.

•

For the first point, we take
u_{0}\in K
and we write

(A.13)

\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}=\Phi(t)u_{0}+\left(\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}-\Phi(t)u_{0}\right)

From Proposition
A.10
, there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}

\sup_{|t|\leq T}\sup_{v_{0}\in K}\left\lVert\Phi_{N}(t)v_{0}-\Phi(t)v_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{\sigma}}\leq\varepsilon

Thus, for all
N\geq N_{0}
,
\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}-\Phi(t)u_{0}\in B_{\varepsilon}^{H^{\sigma}}
for all
|t|\leq T
and all
u_{0}\in K
. Coming back to (
A.13
), it implies that for all
N\geq N_{0}

\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}\in\Phi(t)(K)+B_{\varepsilon}^{H^{\sigma}}

for all
u_{0}\in K
and all
|t|\leq T
.

•

The second point is a consequence of the first one. Let
|t|\leq T
. From the continuity of the map
\Phi(t)
(see Proposition
A.8
) we have that
\Phi(t)(K)
is a compact subset of
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
. So from the first point there exists
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
N\geq N_{1}

\Phi_{N}(-\tau)\left(\Phi(t)(K)\right)\subset\Phi(-\tau)\Phi(t)(K)+B_{\varepsilon}^{H^{\sigma}}

for all
|\tau|\leq T
. In particular, for
\tau=t
we have :

\Phi_{N}(-t)\left(\Phi(t)(K)\right)\subset\Phi(-t)\Phi(t)(K)+B_{\varepsilon}^{H^{\sigma}}=K+B_{\varepsilon}^{H^{\sigma}}

Applying
\Phi_{N}(t)
to this we obtain

\Phi(t)(K)\subset\Phi_{N}(t)\left(K+B_{\varepsilon}^{H^{\sigma}}\right)

This concludes the proof of Corollary
A.11
.
∎

A.3.
Structure of the truncated flow

We set

\displaystyle E_{N}

\displaystyle:=\Pi_{N}\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle E_{N}^{\perp}

\displaystyle:=\Pi_{N}^{\perp}\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})=(Id-\Pi_{N})\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})

Proposition A.12
.

We have the following properties ;

(1)

The truncated flow
\Phi_{N}(t)
commute with the frequency projector
\Pi_{N}
, that is

\Phi_{N}(t)\circ\Pi_{N}=\Pi_{N}\circ\Phi_{N}(t)

As a consequence, the truncated flow
\Phi_{N}(t)
maps the finite-dimensional space
E_{N}
to itself. Moreover, the induced map

\begin{split}\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t):\hskip 2.84544pt&E_{N}\longrightarrow E_{N}\\
&u_{0}\longmapsto\Phi_{N}(t)(u_{0})\end{split}

is the flow of the ODE

(FNLS)

\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{2}u=\Pi_{N}\left(|u|^{4}u\right)\\
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}\in E_{N}\end{cases}

which can be seen as the finite-dimensional Hamiltonian equation on
E_{N}
:

\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}u=\frac{\partial H_{N}}{\partial\overline{u}}(u)\\
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}\in E_{N}\end{cases}

with Hamiltonian
H_{N}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\partial_{x}^{2}u\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\frac{1}{6}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{6}(\mathbb{T})}^{6}
, for
u\in E_{N}
.

(2)

The truncated flow
\Phi_{N}(t)
commute with
\Pi_{N}^{\perp}
, that is

\Phi_{N}(t)\circ\Pi_{N}^{\perp}=\Pi_{N}^{\perp}\circ\Phi_{N}(t)

As a consequence, the truncated flow
\Phi_{N}(t)
maps the space
E_{N}^{\perp}
to itself. Moreover, the induced map

\begin{split}\Phi^{\perp}_{N}(t):\hskip 2.84544pt&E_{N}^{\perp}\longrightarrow E_{N}^{\perp}\\
&u_{0}\longmapsto\Phi_{N}(t)(u_{0})\end{split}

is the solution of the linear Schrödinger equation

\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{2}u=0\\
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}\in E_{N}^{\perp}\end{cases}

Hence,
\Phi^{\perp}_{N}(t)
coincide with the linear operator
e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}
on
E_{N}^{\perp}
.

(3)

The truncated flow
\Phi_{N}(t)
can be factorized as
(\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t),e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}})
on
E_{N}\times E_{N}^{\perp}
. In other words, for every
u_{0}\in H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
,

\Phi_{N}(t)(u_{0})=\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t)\Pi_{N}u_{0}+e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u_{0}

Let us now prove these three points.

Proof.

Let
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.

(1)

•
\Phi_{N}(t)
is the flow of (
2.1
). And, when we apply
\Pi_{N}
to equation (
2.1
), we see that
\Pi_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}
is the solution of the equation

\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{2}u=\Pi_{N}\left(|\Pi_{N}u|^{4}\Pi_{N}u\right)=\Pi_{N}\left(|u|^{4}u\right)\\
u|_{t=0}=\Pi_{N}u_{0}\end{cases}

On the other hand, from the definition of the flow of (
2.1
), the solution of the equation above is none other than
\Phi_{N}(t)\Pi_{N}u_{0}
itself. This means that

\Pi_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}=\Phi_{N}(t)\Pi_{N}u_{0}

• Next, we show that (
FNLS
) can be seen as a finite-dimensional Hamiltonian equation on
E_{N}
. Every element
u\in E_{N}
can be decompose as

u=v+iw

where

\begin{split}v&=\sum_{|k|\leq N}\textnormal{Re}(u_{k})e^{ikx}=:\sum_{|k|\leq N}v_{k}e^{ikx}\in E_{N}\\
w&=\sum_{|k|\leq N}\textnormal{Im}(u_{k})e^{ikx}=:\sum_{|k|\leq N}w_{k}e^{ikx}\in E_{N}\end{split}

Furthermore, we invoke the operators

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{u}_{k}}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{k}}+i\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{k}}\\
&\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{u}}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial v}+i\frac{\partial}{\partial w}=:\sum_{|k|\leq N}e^{ikx}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{k}}+i\sum_{|k|\leq N}e^{ikx}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{k}}\end{split}

and also the function

\begin{split}H_{N}:\hskip 2.84544pt&E_{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\\
&u\longmapsto\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\partial_{x}^{2}u\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\frac{1}{6}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{6}(\mathbb{T})}^{6}\end{split}

By performing elementary computations, we see that we can rewrite (
FNLS
) as

(FNLS)

\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}u=\frac{\partial H_{N}}{\partial\overline{u}}(u)\\
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}\end{cases}

This means that (
FNLS
) is an Hamiltonian equation on
E_{N}
with associated Hamiltonian
H_{N}
.

(2)

Once again, applying
\Pi_{N}^{\perp}
to equation (
2.1
), we see that
\Pi_{N}^{\perp}\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}
is the solution of the equation

\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{2}u=0\\
u|_{t=0}=\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u_{0}\end{cases}

This means that

(A.14)

\Pi_{N}^{\perp}\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}=e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u_{0}

Furthermore,
\Pi_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u_{0}
is the solution of the equation

\begin{cases}i\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}^{2}u=\Pi_{N}\left(|\Pi_{N}u|^{4}\Pi_{N}u\right)\\
u|_{t=0}=0\end{cases}

Thus,
\Pi_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u_{0}
is none other than
0
, and we obtain from (
A.14
) that

\Phi_{N}(t)\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u_{0}=\Pi_{N}^{\perp}\Phi_{N}(t)\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u_{0}=e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u_{0}=\Pi_{N}^{\perp}\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}

(3)

As a consequence of the two previous points, we can write

\begin{split}\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}&=\Pi_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}+\Pi_{N}^{\perp}\Phi_{N}(t)u_{0}\\
&=\Phi_{N}(t)\Pi_{N}u_{0}+\Phi_{N}(t)\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u_{0}\\
&=\underbrace{\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t)\Pi_{N}u_{0}}_{\in E_{N}}+\underbrace{e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}\Pi_{N}^{\perp}u_{0}}_{\in E_{N}^{\perp}}\end{split}

∎
